**Must-answer questions missing (10 of 23 gaps):**
- I take levothyroxine (or carbimazole, HRT, metformin, an SSRI, blood thinners). Do I keep taking it during the programme, and will your herbal medicines interact with it? — _Most women aged 40–70 with thyroid problems, menopause or PCOS already take prescription drugs. Fear of stopping thyroid medication or combining herbs with HRT is the most common reason not to book. The page only says 'Bring: … medications'._ Source: needs physician/owner (Dr. Deepu John): policy on continuing prescribed drugs, and screening for herb–drug interactions
- I have a history of breast cancer, or take tamoxifen or an aromatase inhibitor, or have fibroids or endometriosis. Are hormone-active Ayurvedic herbs safe for me? — _Many women in this age group who seek help for menopause are breast-cancer survivors, and their oncologist will ask this question. The page names no exclusions._ Source: needs physician/owner
- Who should NOT take this programme, e.g. pregnant or trying to conceive, uncontrolled hyperthyroidism, heavy or abnormal bleeding, severe osteoporosis, heart disease? — _The page lists 'Fertility Challenges' and 'Hyperthyroidism' as suitable and plans Virechanam and Vasti, but states no contraindications. Patients and their doctors at home need exclusion criteria before trusting a hospital._ Source: needs physician/owner
- Which tests do you run or need (TSH/T3/T4, FSH/LH, estradiol, AMH, HbA1c, lipids, ultrasound)? Are they done on site, how recent must my reports be, and what do the tests cost? — _The page says therapies depend on 'hormone level', tells patients to bring 'recent reports', and says lab tests 'will cost extra', but gives no test list, no lab location and no price._ Source: needs physician/owner; this page only says 'recent reports' and 'lab tests' are extra
- How soon should I notice a change in hot flushes, cycle regularity, energy or my thyroid results, and how is progress measured at discharge? — _Paying about ₹4.8 lakh upfront for 21 nights, a patient wants a realistic idea of what counts as success. The page gives no timeline and no outcome measures._ Source: needs physician/owner (no outcome figures may be invented)
- What exactly does the fee cover? Are Panchakarma procedures, internal medicines and lab tests included or extra, and how much should I budget for extras? — _The page contradicts itself ('All-Inclusive… No hidden fees' vs medicines, tests and Panchakarma as extras) and contradicts /faqs-downloads/ q-18. With 100% advance payment, this blocks bookings._ Source: needs physician/owner; after the ruling, align this page with /faqs-downloads/ q-18
- Why is 21 nights the minimum, can I do 14, and what is the total price for my villa? — _Three weeks off work is a big commitment. The page shows per-night rates and 'Minimum Duration is 21 Nights' but no total, e.g. ₹22,780 × 21 = ₹478,380 for a Deluxe single. That total is computed from figures on the page._ Source: this page (per-night rate × 21 nights) + /faqs-downloads/ q-05 and q-14; any shorter stay needs physician/owner
- Will female therapists treat me, and how is modesty handled during oil therapies, Vasti and Udwarthanam? — _This decides the booking for Middle Eastern patients and matters to many European and Russian women too. It concerns intimate procedures such as Vasti. The page is silent._ Source: needs physician/owner (same-gender therapist policy); /faqs-downloads/ q-55 covers only dress code and draping
- Who will my physician be? Qualifications, experience in women's health, can I ask for a female physician, and which languages do the doctors speak (English, German, French, Russian, Arabic)? — _No physician is named on the page, and it says 'Doctor might change as subject to availability.' International patients want to know who treats them, and whether they can do it in their own language._ Source: /faqs-downloads/ q-06 and q-11 (BAMS-qualified physicians, Chief Physician oversight); named women's-health physician, female-physician option and languages need physician/owner
- What happens after discharge? Can my herbal medicines be shipped to Germany, the UAE, the US or Russia? Will you send a discharge summary to my endocrinologist or gynaecologist, and when are follow-ups? — _Hormonal conditions are long-term. The page promises a 'Continuous post-discharge wellness plan' but gives no details._ Source: /faqs-downloads/ q-39, q-40, q-41 (online follow-ups at 1, 3 and 6 months) and q-58 (discharge summary); shipping medicines abroad and customs need owner

**For Dr. Deepu John:** 'This Ayurvedic hormonal balance treatment aims to restore hormonal balance and enhance the well-being of the endocrine system': an outcome claim · Hero, meta description and OG description: 'Rejuvenate your endocrine health naturally': outcome claim plus 'naturally' implying safety · 'A sattvic diet, guided meditation, and lifestyle guidance additionally aid in endocrine revitalization': outcome claim · '...reclaiming balance, energy, emotional health, and natural rhythm—softly and entirely': 'entirely' implies complete resolution · 'Who is This Program For?' list: claims the programme suits Hypothyroidism AND Hyperthyroidism, 'Fertility Challenges Linked to Hormonal Issues', 'Women Recovering from Hormonal Therapy or Contraceptive Use', 'Unexplained Weight Gain' and 'Anxiety', with no exclusion criteria. Needs contraindications: pregnancy or trying to conceive, uncontrolled hyperthyroidism, hormone-sensitive cancer history, abnormal bleeding, and a rule that unexplained weight gain is investigated before admission · Process step 04: 'Treatment therapies will include … virechanam, vasti, matravasti … based on your body type, condition and hormone level'. It commits to specific Panchakarma procedures and implies hormone testing · Herbal medicines: 'made from natural herbs to support the internal healing process and strengthen the body'. Implies safety without addressing interactions with levothyroxine, antithyroid drugs, HRT, tamoxifen/aromatase inhibitors, metformin or anticoagulants, and gives no guidance on continuing prescribed medication · The FAQ answer, and any rewrite of it: 'The treatment usually involves detoxification…'; also the 'Women’s hormone detox program' framing · 'Practising Yoga Asanas with Bandhas, Pranayama, and yoga nidra each day': bandhas carry contraindications (menstruation, pregnancy, prolapse, uncontrolled hypertension) that the page does not state · Daily routine: 'detoxifying drink to kickstart the metabolism', and Udwarthana 'to promote detoxification'. Inclusions: 'herbal steam baths to restore balance'. Physiological claims, and steam suitability for heat-intolerant menopausal or hyperthyroid patients · 'Testimonials — Transformative Results': an outcome heading over testimonials that are not about this condition · Commitments to 'Daily Consultation', 'Follow-up and Monitoring' and a 'Continuous post-discharge wellness plan'. The physician must confirm what is actually delivered for this programme · 'Minimum Duration is 21 Nights' as the clinical minimum for this condition. Confirm it is a physician-set clinical minimum and say whether any exceptions apply

### /preventive-and-regenerative-treatment/ — weak

Preventive health and general rejuvenation for broadly well adults. The page also lists "Stress-related disorders". It is not a disease-specific programme. What the content actually delivers: a 3-night minimum; one therapy a day (examples given: Abhyanga, Udwarthana, herbal steam); a daily doctor check-in; daily group yoga and meditation; a vegetarian or vegan sattvic diet; weekly workshops. Panchakarma, internal medicines and lab tests are paid add-ons. /faqs-downloads/ q-14 calls it "an introduction to Ayurveda". For the sitemap flow: in the site-tree group "Rejuvenation & detoxification" this is the entry-level (shortest, lightest) programme. It belongs before /immunity-enhancement-executive-health-assessment-therapy/ (7-night minimum, 2 therapies a day), /holistic-treatment-for-rejuvenation-and-detoxification/ and /panchakarma-therapy/. "Detox" in its name promises more than it contains. Its promise ("Boost immunity") overlaps the Immunity page, so a visitor cannot tell the two apart. Snapshot source: english-candidate/prerender/preventive-and-regenerative-treatment.html (mobile SSR; scripts stripped). The FAQPage JSON-LD lives in public/ahv/views/ahv-page-preventive-regenerative-{desktop,mobile}.json and matches the 4 visible FAQs word for word, so any FAQ fix must change both views.

FAQ questions on the page: 4.

**Proofreading (verified):**
- [locked-word] “Here are the questions guests most often ask before starting the preventive health & regenerative programme.” — Locked word "guests" in the FAQ introduction. **Fix:** Here are the questions patients most often ask before starting the Preventive Health & Regenerative programme.
- [wrong-fact] “The World Health Organization reports traditional medicine in use across more than 170 of its 194 member states.” — No source is given, and "more than 170" appears to overstate it. WHO's 2019 Global Report on Traditional and Complementary Medicine says 170 Member States (88%) reported use. The statistic also says nothing about this programme. **Fix:** Check it against the WHO 2019 report and, if kept, write "170 of its 194 Member States (WHO, 2019)" with a link. Otherwise delete the sentence.
- [grammar] “Preventive & Regenerative wellness program is a holistic approach rooted in Ayurvedic traditions, focusing on proactive health measures and rejuvenation.” — Missing article ("A/The"). The answer is circular and says nothing about what the programme is. It also uses a different programme name from the H1. **Fix:** The Preventive Health & Regenerative programme is a doctor-guided Ayurvedic admission of at least 3 nights: a daily consultation, one prescribed therapy a day, a vegetarian diet, and daily yoga and meditation. (Every fact here is already on the page.)
- [unclear] “This Natural Preventive Health Program is beneficial for individuals seeking overall wellness, preventive healthcare, and natural rejuvenation.” — A third name for the programme; "package" in the question breaks the clinical register. It leaves out "Stress-related disorders", which the page lists under "Who is This Program For?", and gives no exclusions. **Fix:** Use one programme name and the word "programme". Add who should not book and who needs the physician's clearance first (needs physician).
- [grammar] “as suggested by the doctor.” — A dangling modifier at the end of FAQ 4; a plan is prescribed, not suggested. **Fix:** …based on your constitution, imbalances and health goals, and prescribed by your physician.
- [locked-word] “Who is This Program For?” — "Program" (US) conflicts with the required register "programme", which the page itself uses in its FAQ intro and CTAs. "This" is wrongly capitalised. The page uses five names for one programme: H1 "Preventive Health & Regenerative Ayurvedic Detox Program"; FAQ "Preventive & Regenerative Wellness Program", "Natural Preventive Health Program", "holistic detox for preventive health program"; step 03 "Regenerative Body Detox Program"; title/JSON-LD "Preventive & Regenerative Ayurvedic Detox at Kairali". **Fix:** Pick one name, spell it "programme" throughout (the H1, the headings and the JSON-LD too), and write "Who is this programme for?".
- [locked-word] “World Luxury Award Winner” — Locked word "luxury" in the trust strip, which appears twice. The owner specified this exact strip text in D-20260916-10, so the directive conflicts with the language lock. **Fix:** Owner ruling needed. Suggested: "International Award Winner" or "Multi-award-winning since 2015".
- [locked-word] “Ayurveda’s time-tested wisdom guides the journey to holistic well-being.” — Locked word "journey". **Fix:** Ayurveda’s time-tested principles guide every stage of your programme.
- [locked-word] “Regenerative Body Detox Program focuses on eliminating body pains and day-to-day stress and provides relaxation to the guest to revitalize the body.” — Locked word "guest". It also overclaims ("eliminating body pains") and gives the programme yet another name. **Fix:** The programme aims to ease everyday stress and physical tension and to help the patient feel restored. (The claim wording goes to Dr. Deepu John.)
- [locked-word] “with continued doctor support for a safe and effective journey.” — Locked word "journey"; "safe and effective" is a guarantee. **Fix:** …with your physician monitoring you throughout the programme.
- [locked-word] “Luxury, vastu-compliant villas with modern amenities” — Locked word "luxury" in the Inclusions list. **Fix:** Private Vastu-designed villas with modern amenities
- [locked-word] “Learn about the journey from seed to medicine.” — Locked word "journey". **Fix:** See how a medicinal plant goes from seed to medicine.
- [locked-word] “Premium luxury experience” — Locked word "luxury" in the Royal Villa tariff tag. **Fix:** Spacious private villa
- [locked-word] “Inclusions — Your Holistic Wellness Journey Includes” — Locked word "journey" in the accordion title. **Fix:** Inclusions — what your programme includes
- [locked-word] “Luxury Stay: Unwind in private, eco-friendly villas designed for tranquility” — Locked word "luxury" (the same accordion's welcome line also says "serene retreat"). **Fix:** Accommodation: private, eco-friendly villas designed for rest and recovery
- [locked-word] “Personalized Care: Daily wellness check-ins with your Ayurvedic doctor to tailor your journey.” — Locked word "journey". **Fix:** Daily check-ins with your Ayurvedic physician to adjust your treatment.
- [locked-word] “Acknowledged for its outstanding service and guest satisfaction” — Locked word "guest" in the site's own prose, not an award name. It appears twice (Certificates of Excellence 2018 and 2019). **Fix:** …outstanding service and patient satisfaction
- [locked-word] “Celebrated as a leading wellness and spa brand” — Locked word "spa" in the site's own prose (South Asian Travel Awards 2018 card). **Fix:** Recognised as a leading Ayurveda and wellness brand
- [locked-word] “socializing with fellow retreat participants.” — Hospitality register ("retreat participants"). **Fix:** socialising with fellow patients.
- [locked-word] “Clients looking for natural rejuvenation” — The required register is "patient", not "client". **Fix:** Patients looking for natural rejuvenation
- [contradiction] “All-Inclusive Pricing: No hidden fees! Includes accommodation, meals, therapies, and applicable taxes.” — Contradicts the same page. Internal medicines, lab tests, Panchakarma, extra food, laundry, telephone, internet and private meditation are all charged extra, and medicine costs "can only be ascertained as your treatment progresses". "No hidden fees" is not true as written. **Fix:** Your tariff includes accommodation, three meals, one therapy a day, daily consultation, yoga, meditation and taxes. Medicines, lab tests, extra therapies (including Panchakarma), laundry and internet are charged separately.
- [contradiction] “Internal Medicines and test reports needed for your treatment can only be ascertained as your treatment progresses and will cost extra.” — Contradicts /faqs-downloads/ q-18, which lists "Herbal medicines and formulations during your stay" as included in packages. **Fix:** Owner must rule whether medicines are included, then align this page, the Inclusions accordion and /faqs-downloads/ q-18.
- [contradiction] “Support: Airport transfers & multilingual staff” — Listed under Inclusions, which implies transfers are included. /faqs-downloads/ q-18 says airport/railway transfers cost extra and gives separate rates. The tariff inclusions list also omits transfers. **Fix:** Support: airport transfers arranged on request (charged separately — see How to Reach). Owner to confirm.
- [contradiction] “The above rates are valid from April 1, 2025, to October 31, 2026.” — Three different validity windows exist: this page (1 Apr 2025–31 Oct 2026); /faqs-downloads/ q-46 ("valid 1 Oct 2026 – 30 Sep 2027"); english-candidate/long-form-programme-set.json (validTo 2026-09-30, used by the programme finder). The page rates expire in about 5 weeks, just before the European winter booking season. **Fix:** Owner to confirm the current rate card and validity dates, then update all three places together.
- [contradiction] “Evening Meditation: Wind down with private guided mindfulness sessions in the evening.” — The next line says "Private sessions are also available at an additional cost", and /faqs-downloads/ q-18 lists private meditation as extra. So the first line wrongly promises private sessions as included. The two Evening Meditation lines duplicate each other. **Fix:** Keep one line: "Evening Meditation: guided group mindfulness or sound sessions; private sessions at extra cost."
- [contradiction] “Daily Abhyangam massages and therapeutic therapies bolster your body’s resilience.” — The package is "Daily 1 Therapies Per Person", but this sentence promises Abhyangam daily plus further therapies. "Therapeutic therapies" is a tautology, and "massages" breaks the clinical register. **Fix:** One physician-prescribed therapy each day, for example Abhyangam (herbal oil therapy).
- [contradiction] “is NABH-accredited for Panchakarma — certificate AH-2024-0250, valid 25 April 2024 through 24 April 2027.” — The trust strip says "NABH Certified Hospital". The award card limits the accreditation to Panchakarma and says "accredited", not "certified", so the two statements of scope disagree. **Fix:** Owner to confirm the exact certificate scope and use one wording everywhere, e.g. "NABH-accredited Ayurvedic hospital" only if the certificate says so.
- [unclear] “Personal Laundry, Telephone Calls, Internet & other additional services requested will be charged extra.” — /faqs-downloads/ q-17 and q-18 say Wi-Fi in common areas is free and included. **Fix:** Owner to confirm, e.g. "Free Wi-Fi in common areas; in-villa internet, laundry and telephone charged extra."
- [overclaim-medical] “Boost immunity, reduce stress & rejuvenate naturally” — The hero, meta description and JSON-LD description all lead with an immunity-boosting claim. **Fix:** Send to Dr. Deepu John. Safer: "A short, doctor-guided Ayurvedic programme to rest, reset daily habits and support everyday well-being."
- [overclaim-medical] “By strengthening the immune system, it reduces the risk of illnesses and boosts the body’s defense mechanisms.” — Claims the programme reduces disease risk. No evidence is cited, and 3 nights with one therapy a day cannot support it. **Fix:** Remove, or reword after physician sign-off.
- [overclaim-medical] “Techniques include in-house herbal oil therapy (Abhyangam) and yoga practices to enhance cellular health and energy flow.” — "Cellular health" is a pseudo-scientific claim. **Fix:** …herbal oil therapy (Abhyangam) and yoga, to help you relax and move more freely.
- [overclaim-medical] “ensuring lasting results and preventive care.” — Guarantees lasting results. **Fix:** …so the programme keeps matching your needs.
- [overclaim-medical] “Ongoing support ensures long-term health and addresses any concerns or needs.” — Guarantees long-term health. The page also never says what the ongoing support is, how long it lasts or what it costs. **Fix:** Online follow-up consultations are available after discharge (/faqs-downloads/ q-41: typically at 1, 3 and 6 months). Owner to confirm for this programme.
- [overclaim-medical] “practical do’s and don’ts to maintain optimal health and prevent diseases naturally.” — A disease-prevention claim. **Fix:** …practical do’s and don’ts for everyday health.
- [overclaim-medical] “Reduces stress, improves mental health, and offers personalized therapy.” — A mental-health outcome claim, mixed with a feature in the same bullet. **Fix:** Physician sign-off. Suggested: "Helps you manage everyday stress, with therapies chosen for you."
- [overclaim-medical] “Zodiac-assigned rooms, designed following Vaastu Shastra principles, enhance your healing experience.” — Claims that zodiac-assigned rooms improve healing, which does not belong on a hospital page. "Vaastu" here but "vastu" later on the same page. **Fix:** Villas are designed following Vastu Shastra principles.
- [grammar] “This preventive approach wards off ailments rejuvenates, promotes longevity, and restores natural health.” — Missing comma after "ailments". "Wards off ailments" and "promotes longevity" are unsupported medical claims. **Fix:** Add the comma, then send the claims to Dr. Deepu John. Suggested: "This preventive approach focuses on rest, routine and balance."
- [overclaim-medical] “Transformative Results” — The heading promises results on the strength of two testimonials. **Fix:** What patients say
- [overclaim-medical] “Stress-related disorders” — Names a clinical indication without scope. It also breaks the list's parallel form (the other items describe people, this names a disorder). **Fix:** People managing everyday stress (physician to define which stress-related conditions suit this programme and which need another programme).
- [typo] “Worlds Luxury Spa Awards Winner 2024” — "Worlds" should be "World"; the 2017–2023 cards spell the body correctly. The heading "Worlds Luxury Hotel Awards 2024" has the same error, and its own body text says "World". **Fix:** World Luxury Spa Awards Winner 2024 / World Luxury Hotel Awards 2024
- [typo] “was honored with the CONSIDICI National Award in 2019.” — The heading says "COSIDICI" but the body says "CONSIDICI". **Fix:** COSIDICI National Award
- [typo] “has been admitted as an ALLIED member of the Association of Tourism Trade Organisation, India” — Heading says "Organisations", body says "Organisation"; the body also has "ALLIED" in capitals, no full stop, and a different brand form ("Kairali Ayurvedic Healing Village"). **Fix:** …has been admitted as an allied member of the Association of Tourism Trade Organisations, India.
- [typo] “South Asian Travel Awards 2018.” — Stray full stop in the heading and the image alt text. **Fix:** South Asian Travel Awards 2018
- [grammar] “Daily 1 Therapies Per Person” — Number agreement (1 … Therapies). **Fix:** One therapy per day, per person
- [typo] “Daily Rejuvenation: Ayurvedic therapies per day, including herbal steam baths to restore balance.” — The count is missing ("__ therapies per day"). The same line is repeated under Mind-Body Practices as "One Ayurvedic therapy per day", so it appears twice. **Fix:** Keep one line under Ayurvedic Therapies: "One Ayurvedic therapy per day, including herbal steam baths." Delete the copy under Mind-Body Practices.
- [grammar] “Bring: Cotton clothes, medications, recent reports & open mind” — Missing article. **Fix:** Bring: cotton clothes, your current medications, recent medical reports and an open mind
- [grammar] “Immerse in Yoga and Meditation in the serene Western Ghats” — "Immerse" needs an object. **Fix:** Immerse yourself in yoga and meditation at the foot of the Western Ghats
- [unclear] “History of Preventive Health & Regenerative Ayurvedic Detox Program” — Missing "the". The section contains no history, only a repeat of benefits, so it fails the patient who opens it. **Fix:** Rename it (e.g. "How Ayurveda approaches prevention"), or have a physician supply real history (e.g. the Swasthavritta tradition) with sources.
- [unclear] “Rejuvenated Health and Vitality at Kairali” — Both testimonials carry the same heading. **Fix:** Give each testimonial its own heading, or drop the headings.
- [grammar] “We came to Kairali for preventive and regenerative therapy to manage my stress and health better.” — Mixes "We" and "my"; a stray "–" follows the testimonial before the names. **Fix:** If this is the patients' verbatim text, keep it (confirm consent) and just remove the stray dash. If it was edited, fix the pronouns with the patients' approval.
- [typo] “The above rates are inclusive of applicable taxes*.” — The asterisk points to no footnote. **Fix:** Remove the asterisk, or add the footnote it refers to (e.g. "Rates subject to change as per government policies").
- [grammar] “Price for Child between the Age of 5 to 12 years” — Grammar. Child add-ons are shown only in INR, while adult add-ons are in INR/EUR/USD. **Fix:** Children aged 5 to 12 (add EUR/USD figures from the owner's rate card)
- [unclear] “Up to less than 15 days before arrival date or Change in Date during Stay – No Refund.” — "Up to less than" is unclear, and the tiers overlap ("Up to 30 days" and "Up to 15 days"). A patient cannot tell which tier covers day 15–29. **Fix:** 30 days or more before admission: full refund. 15–29 days: 50% refund. Fewer than 15 days, or a date change during your admission: no refund. (Owner to confirm the exact day boundaries; /faqs-downloads/ q-51 overlaps at day 30.)
- [grammar] “Abstinence from Smoking, Alcohol, & Coffee is strictly adhered to.” — A rule is enforced, not "adhered to"; the capitalisation is random. **Fix:** Smoking, alcohol and coffee are not permitted during your programme.
- [grammar] “Doctor might change as subject to availability.” — Ungrammatical. The page names no doctor, so this disclaimer refers to nothing. **Fix:** Your consulting physician may change, subject to availability. (Better: name the physicians. Needs owner.)

**Must-answer questions missing (10 of 21 gaps):**
- I take long-term medication for blood pressure, cholesterol, diabetes or thyroid (or a blood thinner). Can I do this programme, and will the doctor tell me to stop or change anything? — _Most 40–70-year-olds who book 'preventive' care are on at least one long-term medicine. The page only says "Bring: ... medications". Fear of herb–drug interactions or being told to stop medication is the top reason such patients do not book._ Source: needs physician/owner (Dr. Deepu John: the policy on existing prescriptions and herb–drug interaction screening). Nothing on the site states it.
- Is it safe for someone my age with heart disease, a past stroke, diabetes, a joint replacement or a cancer history? Who should NOT book this programme? — _FAQ 2 says only that it suits 'individuals seeking overall wellness'. It gives no contraindications, age guidance or comorbidity rules, yet a page promising prevention attracts exactly these patients._ Source: /faqs-downloads/ q-35 (senior patients: modified treatments, mobility help, medical monitoring, emergency services on call) for the general part. Contraindications specific to this programme need physician.
- How is this different from Immunity Enhancement & Executive Health Assessment, Complete Rejuvenation & Detoxification, and Panchakarma — which should a healthy 55-year-old choose? — _Four sibling programmes sit in the same 'Rejuvenation & detoxification' group, and this page and the Immunity page both promise to 'boost immunity'. Without a comparison the patient stalls or picks the wrong one. The content shows this is the lightest option (3 nights, 1 therapy a day); the Immunity programme is 7 nights and 2 therapies a day._ Source: Sibling pages /immunity-enhancement-executive-health-assessment-therapy/, /holistic-treatment-for-rejuvenation-and-detoxification/, /panchakarma-therapy/, plus /faqs-downloads/ q-14 (recommended durations). The rule for recommending one over another needs physician.
- Is this 'detox' Panchakarma (purging, enemas), or something gentler — and what exactly will my one daily therapy be? — _The name says 'Detox'; the inclusions make Panchakarma a paid add-on. Older patients fear intensive purification, and others expect it. Either way, the ambiguity costs trust._ Source: This page (Panchakarma is an add-on; example therapies: Abhyanga, Udwarthana, herbal steam) and /faqs-downloads/ q-49 (one therapy a day for this programme). The list of therapies typically prescribed needs physician.
- Is 3 nights worth a long-haul flight? How soon will I notice anything, and how long do most international patients stay? — _FAQ 3 gives only the minimum. A patient flying 8–12 hours from Europe, the USA or Russia weighs the flight against the benefit, and 3 nights includes jet-lag recovery._ Source: /faqs-downloads/ q-14 (3 nights = 'an introduction to Ayurveda'). What patients can realistically expect, and whether longer stays are advised for long-haul arrivals, needs physician (no outcome promises).
- Does 'preventive health' include any medical check-up — blood tests, ECG, blood pressure and BMI baseline — and is it in the price? — _To a European, US or Gulf patient aged 40–70, 'preventive health' means screening. The page says lab tests are extra, and the sibling programme is literally called 'Executive Health Assessment'._ Source: needs physician/owner (which tests are done or offered, and their cost). The sibling /immunity-enhancement-executive-health-assessment-therapy/ page is the comparison point.
- What will my total bill be, including medicines and tests? Can I get an estimate before paying 100% in advance? — _The terms demand 100% advance payment while saying medicine and test costs 'can only be ascertained as your treatment progresses'. The same inclusions block says 'No hidden fees', and /faqs-downloads/ q-18 says medicines are included. That contradiction is a direct booking blocker._ Source: needs owner (a ruling on whether medicines are included, plus a typical cost range or a pre-arrival estimate policy for this programme). Payment methods: /faqs-downloads/ q-34, q-50.
- Who are the doctors — names, qualifications, years of experience — and will they speak my language (German, French, Russian, Arabic)? — _The page names no physician, adds 'Doctor might change as subject to availability', and claims only 'multilingual staff'. Older international patients choose a doctor as much as a hospital._ Source: /faqs-downloads/ q-11 (BAMS/MD Ayurveda qualifications in general). The named physicians, their credentials and the languages they speak need owner.
- If I fall ill or have a cardiac event there, how far is the nearest modern (allopathic) hospital, and who takes me? — _This is critical for 40–70-year-olds with comorbidities travelling to rural Palakkad, and the page says nothing about it._ Source: /faqs-downloads/ q-37 (arrangements with nearby hospitals; evacuation insurance advised) and q-35 (emergency services on call). The named hospital, its distance and the transfer arrangement need owner.
- What happens after I go home — follow-up consultations, medicines shipped to my country, a discharge summary for my GP — and what does it cost? — _'Preventive' only works if it continues at home. The page promises a 'Continuous post-discharge wellness plan' and 'Ongoing support' without saying what, how long or at what cost._ Source: /faqs-downloads/ q-39, q-40, q-41 (online follow-ups typically at 1, 3 and 6 months) and q-58 (discharge summary). Follow-up cost and international shipping of medicines (customs) need owner.

**For Dr. Deepu John:** Hero, meta description and JSON-LD WebPage description: "Boost immunity, reduce stress & rejuvenate naturally" is the page's headline promise, and it is an immunity claim. · "By strengthening the immune system, it reduces the risk of illnesses and boosts the body’s defense mechanisms." and "Natural rejuvenation enhances vitality, energy, and immune strength." both claim reduced disease risk and immune effects. · "This preventive approach wards off ailments rejuvenates, promotes longevity, and restores natural health." and "supporting long-term wellness and longevity" are longevity and disease-prevention claims. · "Stress-related disorders" is listed as a target group. The physician must define which stress-related conditions a 3-night, one-therapy programme suits, and which are excluded (e.g. patients on psychiatric medication). · "Reduces stress, improves mental health" is a mental-health outcome claim. · Process step 03: "focuses on eliminating body pains" and "to enhance cellular health and energy flow" claim pain elimination and cellular effects. · Outcome and safety guarantees: "ensuring lasting results" (step 05), "Ongoing support ensures long-term health" (Post Treatment), and "for a safe and effective journey" (During Treatment). · "prevent diseases naturally" (Sunday session), "addresses the root causes of potential health issues" (History), and Abhyangam/Shirodhara "enhance circulation, relax the mind, and stimulate healing". · 'Detox' framing: the programme name, "promoting detoxification through ayurvedic therapies", Udwarthana "to promote detoxification" and "detoxifying drink to kickstart the metabolism" all promise detox while Panchakarma is excluded. The physician must define what detoxification a one-therapy-a-day programme delivers, or the name must change. · "Zodiac-assigned rooms ... enhance your healing experience" claims healing from zodiac/Vastu room allocation. · Diet and abstinence: 'lean protein sources' against 'vegetarian or vegan', plus the coffee ban. The physician must confirm the prescribed diet wording, diabetic and other therapeutic diets, and caffeine-withdrawal guidance for 40–70-year-old patients. · The daily routine (06:30 yoga for 30–45 min, nature walk, farm visits) is presented as standard. The physician must confirm it is adapted or optional for older patients and those with comorbidities; the footnote only partly covers this. · Testimonials under "Transformative Results" ("life-changing", "boost immunity ... did just that", "My body feels lighter, my mind is clearer") are used as outcome evidence. They need physician review and confirmation of patient consent. · All 4 FAQ answers and the matching FAQPage JSON-LD in ahv-page-preventive-regenerative-{desktop,mobile}.json: any rewrite of who can benefit, contraindications or duration is clinical content, and both views must stay identical to the visible FAQ. · Post-discharge claims ("Continuous post-discharge wellness plan"; follow-ups "assess the effectiveness"): the physician and owner must confirm what follow-up this programme actually includes (/faqs-downloads/ q-41 says 1, 3 and 6 months online) and what it costs. · FAQ intro statistic (WHO, "more than 170 of its 194 member states") is a factual health claim that needs a source. The WHO 2019 report states 170.

### /treatment-for-diabetes/ — weak

Diabetes & Metabolic Balance Therapy (Madhumeha Chikitsa): a residential programme for type 2 diabetes, pre-diabetes and insulin resistance. It combines physician consultation, Panchakarma (Virechana, Basti), herbal medicines, therapies, diet, yoga and meditation. The rates reckoner sets a 14-night minimum; the page says '14/21/28-day'.

FAQ questions on the page: 4.

**Proofreading (verified):**
- [locked-word] “Can Ayurvedic treatment cure diabetes completely?” — The FAQ question uses the banned word 'cure' on a KTAHV clinical page. It also makes a full cure of diabetes sound like a realistic outcome. **Fix:** Reword the question without the banned word, for example 'Will this programme make my diabetes go away?'. The answer must come from Dr. Deepu John (see clinical risk).
- [overclaim-medical] “While Ayurvedic treatment can effectively manage diabetes, a complete cure depends on various factors such as individual response, adherence to treatment, and lifestyle modifications.” — This says a complete cure is possible under the right conditions. That is a medical overclaim, and dangerous for type 1 and insulin-treated patients. It also uses the banned word 'cure' again. **Fix:** Dr. Deepu John to write an answer that says the programme supports management, does not replace diabetes medication and promises no remission. Keep banned words out.
- [overclaim-medical] “Yes, Ayurvedic treatment is generally safe for diabetic individuals.” — The answer gives a blanket 'Yes' on safety. It does not mention the hypoglycaemia risk of glucose-lowering herbs, fasting or purgation taken alongside insulin or sulfonylureas, or any contraindications. **Fix:** Replace it with a physician-approved statement of what the physician checks before admission (current medicines, kidney function, hypo risk) and who is not suitable. Use 'people with diabetes' rather than 'diabetic individuals'. Needs Dr. Deepu John.
- [overclaim-medical] “which may lead to a reduction in the need for diabetes medications” — This implies a medicine reduction on a YMYL page. 'Your healthcare provider' is ambiguous: it does not say whether Kairali's physician or the patient's own diabetologist changes the doses. **Fix:** Dr. Deepu John to define who reviews and adjusts glucose-lowering medicines during and after admission. State plainly that patients never reduce or stop them on their own.
- [contradiction] “It is typically recommended to undergo an initial intensive treatment period of a few weeks or months, followed by a maintenance phase.” — This contradicts the page's own 'This 14/21/28-day program' and 'Villas — Minimum Duration is 14 Nights'. The RATES_RECKONER lists this programme at 14 nights minimum. **Fix:** State the 14-night minimum and say the physician recommends the length at consultation. The owner must confirm that 21 and 28 nights are offered.
- [contradiction] “This 14/21/28-day program also includes Yoga, fitness classes, Ayurvedic cooking workshops, and personalized diet plans for continued health at home.” — The page mixes days and nights (the tariff says '14 Nights'). 'Fitness classes' appears nowhere in the inclusions. 'Program' should be 'programme' (required register). **Fix:** Use nights, matching the tariff. Remove 'fitness classes' unless the owner confirms them. Use 'programme'.
- [locked-word] “Here are the questions guests most often ask before starting the diabetes and metabolic balance programme.” — Banned word 'guests' in the FAQ intro. **Fix:** Replace 'guests' with 'patients'.
- [unclear] “Ayurvedic Detox for Diabetes aims to improve blood sugar control” — The FAQ and the About section call the programme 'Ayurvedic Detox for Diabetes', but the H1 names it 'Diabetes & Metabolic Balance Therapy'. Patients comparing names will be confused. **Fix:** Use 'Diabetes & Metabolic Balance Therapy' consistently.
- [wrong-fact] “Diabetes is a lifestyle disease that often goes unnoticed.” — Medically inaccurate: type 1 diabetes is autoimmune, not a lifestyle disease. Only type 2 is strongly linked to lifestyle. **Fix:** Physician to reword so it is specific to type 2, for example 'Type 2 diabetes is closely linked to lifestyle and often goes unnoticed…'. Needs Dr. Deepu John.
- [typo] “It uses Ayurvedic medication, purification treatments, and massages like Thakaradhara, Pizhichil, and Abhyangam.” — 'Thakaradhara' is misspelled (usually Takradhara/Thakradhara), and it is a pouring therapy, not a massage. These three therapies also differ from the Process list (Abhyangam, Shirodhara, Udwarthanam) and the daily routine (Abhyanga, Udwarthana). **Fix:** Physician to confirm which therapies this programme actually uses. Then use one spelling and one list across About, Process and Routine.
- [overclaim-medical] “has successfully treated over 10,000 diabetic patients using a holistic approach” — An outcome and volume claim with no source anywhere in the fact files. 'Successfully' implies a clinical result. **Fix:** Remove it unless hospital records support it. Needs owner and physician sign-off.
- [overclaim-medical] “Instead of merely symptom management, we target the root causes of diabetes.” — A root-cause treatment claim for a chronic metabolic disease. **Fix:** Physician to reword, for example 'we address diet, digestion, activity and stress alongside your existing care'.
- [locked-word] “Our goal is to empower clients with a deeper understanding of diabetes management through Ayurveda.” — 'Clients' is outside the required clinical register. **Fix:** Replace 'clients' with 'patients'.
- [locked-word] “World Luxury Award Winner” — Banned word 'Luxury' in a trust chip that the page itself wrote, not an award's proper name. It appears twice (hero strip and the bottom strip). **Fix:** Replace it with a clinical trust signal (for example 'NABH-accredited · AH-2024-0250'), or get an owner ruling.
- [contradiction] “NABH Certified Hospital” — The awards block on the same page says 'NABH-accredited for Panchakarma — certificate AH-2024-0250'. The chip's 'Certified' and its whole-hospital scope contradict that. **Fix:** Use 'NABH-accredited (Panchakarma)'. The compliance owner must confirm the scope wording.
- [locked-word] “Celebrated as a leading wellness and spa brand, Kairali – The Ayurvedic Healing Village was honored at the South Asian Travel Awards 2018.” — Banned word 'spa' in page-written copy, not an award name. **Fix:** Use 'Kairali – The Ayurvedic Healing Village was honoured at the South Asian Travel Awards 2018.'
- [locked-word] “Acknowledged for its outstanding service and guest satisfaction” — Banned word 'guest', twice (Certificate of Excellence 2018 and 2019 blurbs). **Fix:** Use 'patient satisfaction'.
- [typo] “Worlds Luxury Spa Awards Winner 2024” — 'Worlds' is wrong; the award is 'World Luxury Spa Awards'. The same error appears in 'Worlds Luxury Hotel Awards 2024'. **Fix:** Correct to 'World Luxury Spa Awards 2024' and 'World Luxury Hotel Awards 2024', subject to the banned-word ruling above.
- [typo] “was honored with the CONSIDICI National Award in 2019.” — The body says 'CONSIDICI' but the heading says 'COSIDICI National Award 2019'. **Fix:** Use 'COSIDICI' in both places.
- [wrong-fact] “Mr. Abhilash K. Ramesh was honored as one of the 40 influential leaders in the wellness industry in 2018.” — The linked article (slug '…most-influential-leaders-under-40-by-asiaone') describes a 'most influential leaders under 40' list, not 'one of the 40 influential leaders'. **Fix:** Use 'was named among the most influential leaders under 40 (AsiaOne, 2018)', after the owner confirms the exact title.
- [wrong-fact] “was recognized as the Wellness Brand of the Year – Service Provider in 2016.” — This card's 'Read more' link goes to the AYUSH Brand of the Year 2015 article (…/ayush-brand-of-the-year-2015-award-to-kairali-ayurvedic-group/), the same link as the next card. **Fix:** Point it at the correct 2016 article, or remove the link.
- [grammar] “has been admitted as an ALLIED member of the Association of Tourism Trade Organisation, India” — Missing full stop and all-caps 'ALLIED'. The body says 'Organisation' but the heading says 'Organisations'. The '2018.' heading nearby also has a stray full stop. **Fix:** Use sentence case, add the full stop, make the names match, and remove the stray period in 'South Asian Travel Awards 2018.'
- [contradiction] “Panchakarma, a series of detoxification therapies, forms an integral part of the treatment.” — Contradiction: the inclusions list Panchakarma as a paid add-on ('specialty therapies (e.g., Panchakarma) available as needed'). A patient cannot tell whether the core of this programme is in the price shown. **Fix:** The owner must state whether Panchakarma and internal medicines are included in this programme's tariff. Then align the Process section, Inclusions and T&C.
- [contradiction] “All-Inclusive Pricing: No hidden fees! Includes accommodation, meals, therapies, and applicable taxes.” — The T&C on this page say 'Internal Medicines and test reports… will cost extra', and laundry and internet are charged. /faqs-downloads/ (q-18) says herbal medicines ARE included. The page and the site contradict each other. **Fix:** Remove 'No hidden fees!'. List the extras plainly, and have the owner settle whether medicines are included site-wide.
- [overclaim-medical] “Customized herbal medications are prescribed to regulate blood sugar levels, enhance pancreatic function, and support overall health.” — Claims about pancreatic function and blood-sugar regulation for herbal medicines. **Fix:** Physician to reword it as supportive care, alongside the patient's existing treatment.
- [overclaim-medical] “Blood Sugar Control: Ayurvedic methods maintain stable blood sugar, reducing dangerous fluctuations.” — A definite glycaemic-control claim. Adding glucose-lowering herbs to existing drugs can actually cause dangerous lows. **Fix:** Physician to rewrite or remove it.
- [overclaim-medical] “Hormonal Balance: Target underlying hormonal issues to restore equilibrium.” — Vague, unsupported claim. It is also unclear which hormones are meant. **Fix:** Remove it, or have the physician specify what is meant.
- [unclear] “Our expert nutritionists provide personalized dietary recommendations to manage diabetes effectively.” — The physician roster lists only BAMS physicians, and no nutritionist is recorded in any fact source. **Fix:** The owner must confirm nutritionist staff. Otherwise say 'your physician prescribes your diet'.
- [locked-word] “with continued doctor support for a safe and effective journey.” — Banned word 'journey'. **Fix:** Use 'throughout your admission'.
- [grammar] “*Doctor might change as subject to availability.*” — Ungrammatical, and the asterisks show literally inside the italics. **Fix:** Use 'Your physician may change, subject to availability.'
- [wrong-fact] “Daily Group Yoga Sessions” — The RATES_RECKONER ruling D-YOGA-PRIVATE says the 'group' notation in the treatment lists is a source-PDF error and must not be carried to any site. 'Morning Group Yoga' in the inclusions has the same problem. **Fix:** Remove 'Group'. The owner must confirm what yoga format the treatment package includes.
- [locked-word] “Luxury, vastu-compliant villas with modern amenities” — Banned word 'Luxury'. **Fix:** Use 'Vastu-compliant villas with modern amenities'.
- [locked-word] “Premium luxury experience” — Banned word 'luxury' in the Royal Villa tariff tagline. **Fix:** Use a descriptive tagline, for example 'Larger villa with additional space'.
- [locked-word] “Luxury Stay: Unwind in private, eco-friendly villas designed for tranquility” — Banned word 'Luxury', plus leisure framing. **Fix:** Use 'Accommodation: private, eco-friendly villas…'.
- [locked-word] “Inclusions — Your Holistic Wellness Journey Includes” — Banned word 'Journey' in the accordion title. **Fix:** Use 'Inclusions — what your programme includes'.
- [locked-word] “Daily wellness check-ins with your Ayurvedic doctor to tailor your journey.” — Banned word 'journey'. **Fix:** Use 'Daily check-ins with your physician to adjust your treatment.'
- [locked-word] “Learn about the journey from seed to medicine.” — Banned word 'journey' (Monday farm visit). **Fix:** Use 'See how herbs are grown and processed into medicine.'
- [locked-word] “socializing with fellow retreat participants” — 'Retreat' and 'participants' are leisure register. The FAQ pass removed 'retreat' on sight. 'Your serene retreat' in the welcome line has the same problem. **Fix:** Use 'with other patients'. Rewrite 'settle into your serene retreat' as 'settle into your villa'.
- [grammar] “Bring: Cotton clothes, medications, recent reports & open mind” — Missing article. It also doesn't say which reports a diabetic patient should bring. **Fix:** Use 'an open mind'. The physician must name the reports (see objection gaps).
- [contradiction] “Support: Airport transfers & multilingual staff” — This sits under 'Inclusions', but transfers are priced separately (RATES_RECKONER transfer table; /faqs-downloads/ q-24). 'Multilingual staff' is unverified: the FAQ pass logged the staff-language question as no-recorded-fact. **Fix:** Use 'Airport transfers on request (charged)'. Remove 'multilingual staff' until the owner confirms which languages.
- [overclaim-medical] “Managing Diabetes Naturally: Success Stories from Kairali Ayurvedic Healing Village” — 'Success Stories' is plural but there is only one testimonial, under an H2 of 'Transformative Results'. The testimonial claims blood-sugar control, which is an outcome claim. **Fix:** Use a neutral heading ('Patient experience'). Verify consent and add a line that individual results vary. Needs physician sign-off.
- [wrong-fact] “The above rates are valid from April 1, 2025, to October 31, 2026.” — The RATES_RECKONER dates the 2025–26 treatment rates April 1 2025 through September 30, 2026, which is seven days after this audit. The INR figures shown (₹22,780/₹42,470…) match the 2026–27 yoga Grid 2 table. No INR treatment list exists in the reckoner. **Fix:** The owner must supply the 2026–27 treatment rate list and the correct validity window before these prices keep running.
- [contradiction] “Reservations will be confirmed against 100% advance payments.” — The RATES_RECKONER T&C say a 40% deposit secures the booking and the balance is due at least 30 days before admission. /cancellation-and-refund-policy/ says 100%. The sources contradict each other. **Fix:** The owner must rule on 40% versus 100%. Then align this page, the cancellation page and the reckoner.
- [wrong-fact] “Up to 30 days before the arrival date – Full Refund.” — 'Up to 30 days before' reads as 'within the last 30 days', the opposite of the reckoner ('30+ Days Before Admission: Full refund'). The next line should read 15–30 days for the 50% refund. **Fix:** Use '30 or more days before admission: full refund · 15–30 days: 50% refund · fewer than 15 days, or a date change during your stay: no refund'.
- [grammar] “Up to less than 15 days before arrival date or Change in Date during Stay – No Refund.” — 'Up to less than' is ungrammatical, and the capitalisation is random. **Fix:** Use 'Fewer than 15 days before admission, or a date change during your stay: no refund.'
- [unclear] “Terms & Conditions — This package will also include” — The accordion title says the terms are things the package includes. It makes no sense. **Fix:** Use 'Terms & conditions'.
- [grammar] “Confirmation would be given on a first come first served basis.” — Wrong tense and missing hyphens and comma. **Fix:** Use 'Bookings are confirmed on a first-come, first-served basis.'
- [grammar] “Abstinence from Smoking, Alcohol, & Coffee is strictly adhered to.” — Awkward and ungrammatical, with random capitals. **Fix:** Use 'Smoking, alcohol and coffee are not permitted during your admission.'
- [unclear] “(Note: Preventive Health & Regenerative Program includes one therapy per day.)” — A note about a different programme appears on the diabetes page. **Fix:** Remove it from this page.
- [typo] “Join our Weekly fun, hands-on Ayurvedic cooking classes by in-house chefs.” — Capital 'W' in the middle of a sentence. **Fix:** Use 'Join our weekly hands-on Ayurvedic cooking classes'.
- [typo] “Deep detox with revitalizing therapies.Restore strength, clarity, and vitality.” — Missing space after the full stop (Complete Rejuvenation card). **Fix:** Add a space: 'therapies. Restore'.
- [typo] “MORE PROGRAMs” — Broken casing, and 'program' spelling instead of the register's 'programme'. **Fix:** Use 'More programmes'.
- [typo] “Madhumeha Chikitsa (Kairali’s Treatment For Diabetes )” — Stray space before the closing bracket and title-case 'For'. The ogTitle also joins the two strings with no separator ('…TherapyMadhumeha Chikitsa'). **Fix:** Use 'Madhumeha Chikitsa (Kairali’s treatment for diabetes)' and add a separator in the ogTitle.
- [overclaim-medical] “Ayurvedic diabetes program with detox, herbal meds, yoga & diet to balance metabolism, control sugar & improve overall health.” — The hero line and meta description use casual wording ('herbal meds', 'control sugar') and the 'program' spelling. They also put a glycaemic-control claim in the search snippet. **Fix:** Use a physician-approved line in the programme register, for example 'A physician-led residential Ayurvedic programme for type 2 diabetes: diet, herbal medicines, therapies and yoga alongside your existing care.'
- [grammar] “Personalized Doctor-Guided Program” — The page mixes 'program' and 'programme' ('Pre-Program', 'Who is This Program For?', 'Discover More Customizable Programs'). The required register is 'programme'. British and American spellings also mix ('Individualised' vs 'Personalized', 'honoured' vs 'honored'). **Fix:** Standardise on 'programme' and one spelling system across the page.

**Must-answer questions missing (6 of 22 gaps):**
- I take metformin, insulin, a sulfonylurea or a GLP-1/SGLT2 drug. Do I keep taking them during admission, and who adjusts my dose if my sugar drops on the new diet, the herbs or Virechana purgation? — _The page prescribes glucose-lowering herbs (bitter melon, fenugreek), purgation, enemas and a diet change. Hypoglycaemia is the biggest fear of an insulin-treated patient aged 55–70. FAQ 4 only says 'consult your healthcare provider'._ Source: needs physician/owner — Dr. Deepu John. The FAQ pass logged 'Can I continue my current (allopathic) medicines during treatment?' as no-recorded-fact (english-candidate/faq-content-2026.json, addition #4).
- Am I suitable? I have type 1 diabetes, or long-standing insulin-dependent type 2, or complications: kidney disease, neuropathy or a foot ulcer, retinopathy, heart disease. — _'Who is this for' lists type 2, pre-diabetes and insulin resistance but no exclusions. Older international patients usually have comorbidities and will not fly 8,000 km without knowing they will be admitted._ Source: needs physician/owner — Dr. Deepu John. FAQ-pass addition #10 ('Which conditions do you NOT treat?') and #11 ('Can I take Panchakarma if I have diabetes or high blood pressure?') are both no-recorded-fact.
- How will my blood sugar be monitored during admission (how often, fasting and after meals, HbA1c at admission and discharge)? Is there an on-site lab, and what happens if I have a hypo at night — is a doctor on call? — _The page says only 'Progress monitoring' and 'Daily Consultation', and lists lab tests as an extra charge. Night-time safety is the deciding factor for a diabetic patient travelling alone._ Source: needs physician/owner. /faqs-downloads/ q-35 ('Emergency medical services on call') and q-37 ('arrangements with nearby hospitals') are generic, and physician night and weekend cover is no-recorded-fact (FAQ-pass addition #8).
- What result can I realistically expect, and when — a lower HbA1c, fewer medicines, weight loss? Do you have measured outcomes from past diabetic patients? — _The page claims '10,000 diabetic patients' and 'Transformative Results' but gives no measured outcome or timeline. A sceptical Western patient will ask for numbers or a plain 'no promise' statement._ Source: needs physician/owner — only audited hospital data could support a figure. Otherwise the physician must write a no-guarantee statement.
- What will 14 nights actually cost in total, including the Panchakarma, internal medicines and lab tests the page lists as extras, plus the airport transfer? And are these prices still valid for my dates? — _The page shows only per-night villa rates. It calls Panchakarma 'integral' and also an add-on, says 'No hidden fees' and 'will cost extra', and its rate validity runs out in days. Uncertainty about price is the most common reason people don't book._ Source: This page gives per-night × 14 (for example Deluxe single ₹22,780/night). Transfer prices are on /faqs-downloads/ q-24. The typical add-on range, whether Panchakarma is included, and the 2026–27 treatment rates need the owner. Showing a bundled price needs an Abilash ruling per RATES_RECKONER.
- Which doctor will treat me — what qualifications, how much diabetes experience — and is a modern-medicine doctor (MBBS/endocrinologist) available if my sugar or blood pressure becomes unstable? — _The page says only 'Ayurvedic doctor' and 'Doctor might change'. International patients and their home doctors want named credentials and to know an allopathic doctor can step in._ Source: /online-consultation/ and the homepage physician team (Dr. Deepu John, BAMS, Chief Physician & Head of Department, 12+ years; Dr. Akhila Oommen; Dr. Rahul R; Dr. Ashika Raj, RMO). No diabetes specialisation is published anywhere, so diabetes experience and MBBS/allopathic cover need the owner or physician.

**For Dr. Deepu John:** FAQ 1: 'Can Ayurvedic treatment cure diabetes completely?' and 'a complete cure depends on various factors…' imply a full cure is achievable. Dr. Deepu John must rewrite this as management with no remission promise, and exclude type 1 explicitly. · FAQ 2: 'Yes, Ayurvedic treatment is generally safe for diabetic individuals.' This is a blanket safety claim. The physician must state the pre-admission checks (medicines, kidney function, hypoglycaemia risk) and the contraindications. · FAQ 4: 'which may lead to a reduction in the need for diabetes medications' is a claim about reducing or stopping medicine. The physician must define who adjusts insulin or oral drugs during and after admission, and say patients never change them on their own. · 'has successfully treated over 10,000 diabetic patients' and 'With over a century of experience' are unsourced volume and outcome claims. They need hospital records or removal. · 'Instead of merely symptom management, we target the root causes of diabetes' is a root-cause treatment claim. · 'We choose herbs like bitter melon, fenugreek, and Indian gooseberry for their anti-diabetic properties, aiding blood sugar regulation and organ protection' is an efficacy and organ-protection claim. It also needs a hypoglycaemia and drug-interaction safety line for patients on insulin or sulfonylureas. · Process step 3: 'regulate blood sugar levels, enhance pancreatic function' is a claim about pancreatic function. · Process step 2: 'Virechana (therapeutic purgation) and Basti (medicated enema)' for diabetic patients. The physician must confirm suitability, fluid and glucose monitoring, and contraindications (on hypoglycaemic drugs, CKD, elderly). The physician must also confirm whether Panchakarma is part of this programme or a paid add-on. · 'Two Ayurvedic therapies per day, including herbal steam baths': the physician must confirm heat and steam safety for patients with diabetic neuropathy, cardiac disease or foot problems. · 'Why' bullets: 'Ayurvedic methods maintain stable blood sugar, reducing dangerous fluctuations', 'Enhanced Insulin Sensitivity: Improve insulin effectiveness', 'Hormonal Balance: Target underlying hormonal issues to restore equilibrium', 'Ayurveda alleviates common diabetes symptoms like thirst and fatigue'. All are outcome claims. · Benefits: 'Steadier blood sugar through detox therapies, herbal medication, and a personalized diet'. Yoga step: 'improve insulin sensitivity'. · 'Diabetes is a lifestyle disease' is medically inaccurate for type 1. The physician must correct it to wording specific to type 2. · The suitability list ('Type 2 diabetic individuals', 'Pre-diabetic individuals', 'Individuals with insulin resistance') has no exclusions. The physician must supply who is NOT eligible; it must not be invented. · The testimonial ('helped naturally control my blood sugar levels', Ms. Rashi) under the H2 'Transformative Results' is an outcome testimonial on a YMYL page. It needs consent and authenticity checks and a statement that results vary. · Diet: the generic routine gives diabetic patients 'fresh juice', 'cooked oats with fruits' and 'freshly prepared juices… after every session'. The physician must approve a diabetes-appropriate menu. · Therapy list: 'massages like Thakaradhara, Pizhichil, and Abhyangam' against Process ('Abhyangam, Shirodhara, Udwarthanam'). The physician must confirm which therapies this programme uses and describe them correctly. · Hero and meta description: 'control sugar' and 'herbal meds' are a glycaemic-control claim in the search snippet. · 'Our expert nutritionists provide personalized dietary recommendations to manage diabetes effectively' is a staffing and clinical-diet claim with no roster evidence. The owner and physician must confirm it.

### /yoga-meditation-training/ — weak

No medical condition. The goal is learning yoga (asana), pranayama and meditation during a residential Ayurvedic stay: 7-night minimum, from ₹150,430, daily group yoga and meditation, 2 therapies a day, one weekly class on yogic life. Page identity is unresolved. The Yoga & Meditation hub card says "For: yoga teacher training". /sitemap/ files the page under "Training & education › Yoga teacher training". Yet the page never mentions teacher training, a certificate, contact hours or a syllabus. It shares 397 of its 442 text lines with /yoga-meditation-program/.

FAQ questions on the page: 6.

The page names no medical condition. It describes a general personalised yoga programme: postures, pranayama, meditation, relaxation and yoga philosophy, "customised to your goals after a doctor's assessment". It gives no indications, contraindications, duration, fee, daily schedule, session count or instructor details. The content is thinner than the title suggests. For the sitemap flow: the content does not support placing this page under "Yoga & meditation > Therapeutic programmes" (sitemap-navigation.json link-073), and the hub /yoga-meditation-programs/ tags its card "For: yoga retreat". Its real content siblings are: /yoga-therapy-individual/ (a Knowledge Centre article describing the same personalised yoga therapy in far more detail), /yoga-and-meditation-package/ (priced, 7-night minimum, breadcrumb goes straight to All Programmes rather than via the Yoga hub), /yoga-based-stress-energy-reset-program/ (therapeutic, for burnout, 7 nights) and /digestive-wellness-gut-health-yoga-therapy/ (therapeutic, for digestion, 14 nights). The page's "Explore more programmes" block links to none of them; it lists only 23 Ayurvedic treatment programmes. The FAQPage JSON-LD (in public/ahv/views/ahv-page-yoga-retreats-*.json, not in the prerender body) matches the 3 visible FAQs word for word, so it publishes non-answers as structured answers.

Consent and voluntary admission need a statement. Owner/legal should also check whether residential treatment of substance-use disorders needs registration under India's Mental Healthcare Act 2017, whose definition of mental illness covers conditions linked to alcohol and drug abuse.
