The 'Daily Rejuvenation' block is also duplicated. **Fix:** 'Two Ayurvedic therapies a day, as prescribed by your physician.' Delete the duplicate block.
- [contradiction] “Evening Meditation: Wind down with private guided mindfulness sessions in the evening.” — Two 'Evening Meditation' lines: one says private sessions are included, the next says 'Private sessions are also available at an additional cost.' **Fix:** Keep one line and state plainly what is included and what is extra.
- [wrong-fact] “Daily Group Yoga Sessions” — Abilash's ruling D-YOGA-PRIVATE (17 Jul 2026) says the 'group' wording in the treatment rate lists is a source-PDF error and must not be carried to any site. Private one-to-one yoga is offered. **Fix:** 'Daily yoga sessions'. The physician adapts them for breathless patients.
- [locked-word] “Luxury, vastu-compliant villas with modern amenities” — Banned word 'luxury'. **Fix:** 'Vastu-compliant villas with air-conditioning and attached bathrooms.'
- [locked-word] “Premium luxury experience” — Banned word 'luxury' in the Royal Villa tariff card. **Fix:** 'Larger villa with additional space.'
- [locked-word] “Luxury Stay: Unwind in private, eco-friendly villas designed for tranquility” — Banned word 'luxury'. 'Unwind' is also hospitality register. **Fix:** 'Accommodation: a private, eco-friendly villa for the length of your admission.'
- [locked-word] “Inclusions — Your Holistic Wellness Journey Includes” — Banned word 'journey'. **Fix:** 'What your programme fee includes'.
- [locked-word] “Personalized Care: Daily wellness check-ins with your Ayurvedic doctor to tailor your journey.” — Banned word 'journey'. **Fix:** 'Daily review with your physician to adjust your treatment.'
- [locked-word] “Learn about the journey from seed to medicine.” — Banned word 'journey', in the Monday farm-visit card. **Fix:** 'See how medicinal plants are grown and prepared.'
- [locked-word] “Here are the questions guests most often ask before starting the breath enhancement & respiratory health programme.” — Banned word 'guests' in the FAQ intro. **Fix:** '…the questions patients most often ask before admission to the respiratory programme.'
- [wrong-fact] “The World Health Organization reports traditional medicine in use across more than 170 of its 194 member states.” — The WHO figure is 170 of 194 member states reporting use, not 'more than 170'. It is also off-topic as the lead-in to a respiratory FAQ. **Fix:** '170 of 194' with a WHO citation, or delete the sentence.
- [locked-word] “Can Ayurvedic bronchial treatment completely cure my condition?” — Banned word 'cure' in the question. The answer then avoids giving a straight yes or no. **Fix:** Ask 'Will this programme resolve my condition completely?' and answer plainly with physician-approved text: chronic conditions such as asthma are managed, not permanently resolved.
- [contradiction] “While results vary for each individual, Ayurvedic treatment can provide long-term relief and significantly improve the quality of life.” — Contradicts the About section ('permanent solution', 'eliminate bronchial diseases', 'Say goodbye'). 'Significantly improve' is itself an outcome claim. **Fix:** Bring the About section down to the FAQ's hedged position, and have the physician approve the outcome wording.
- [overclaim-medical] “Unlike conventional medicine, Ayurveda focuses on restoring balance to the body and mind, promoting overall well-being rather than solely treating symptoms.” — Implies conventional medicine only treats symptoms. It also never says whether this programme replaces or works alongside the patient's pulmonologist care, which is what a patient needs to know. **Fix:** 'Ayurvedic care is intended to work alongside, not replace, the treatment your own doctor prescribes.' Physician sign-off.
- [grammar] “Ayurvedic Treatment for Respiratory Problems is a holistic approach” — Random title case in the middle of a sentence. **Fix:** 'Ayurvedic treatment for respiratory problems is…'
- [overclaim-medical] “Ayurvedic treatments are generally safe when administered by qualified practitioners.” — A blanket safety claim that does not address the real risks for this condition: herb–drug interactions, steam or oil triggering bronchospasm, acute attacks. Also says 'doctor' (singular) where the rest of the page says 'doctors'. **Fix:** Physician-written answer covering medication continuation, screening at admission, and emergency arrangements.
- [contradiction] “Typically, the program may range from a week to four weeks.” — Four different durations for the same programme: About '7 to 21-day', this FAQ '1–4 weeks', the tariff 'Minimum Duration is 14 Nights' (which matches the 2025–26 rate list), and /faqs-downloads/ 'from 7 to 14 nights depending on the condition' for respiratory. **Fix:** Standardise on 'minimum 14 nights; your physician confirms the length after consultation'. Any maximum needs physician/owner.
- [grammar] “The duration of the natural therapy for bronchial health program varies” — Awkward name for the programme. The page also mixes 'program' and 'programme', and KTAHV register uses 'programme'. **Fix:** 'The length of the respiratory programme varies…', and use 'programme' throughout ('About the Programme', 'Customisable Programmes').
- [typo] “MORE PROGRAMs” — Broken capitalisation and the US spelling. **Fix:** 'MORE PROGRAMMES'.
- [grammar] “These created therapies for me that included massages, certain breathing methods, and inhalations of herbal steam” — Ungrammatical ('These created'). **Fix:** 'They created therapies for me that included…' Only after the testimonial's source and consent are verified.
- [grammar] “I came about Kairali while searching for a natural remedy for my bronchitis.” — Wrong idiom. **Fix:** 'I came across Kairali…'
- [unclear] “Natural Solutions for Respiratory Health” — All four testimonials use this same H3. Reviewers have first names only and all rated 5.0, with no source, date or consent. HARD GATE 6 forbids presenting unverified reviews as genuine. **Fix:** Owner to confirm each testimonial's source and consent. Give each a distinct heading or none, and add the source/date.
- [overclaim-medical] “Kairali’s Ayurvedic method helped me in ways that traditional therapies were never able to.” — A comparative medical claim against conventional treatment, published by the hospital through a testimonial. **Fix:** Remove this sentence, or have the physician/owner decide whether it may stay.
- [wrong-fact] “The above rates are valid from April 1, 2025, to October 31, 2026.” — The 2025–26 rate PDFs (docs/rates/RATES_RECKONER.md) say valid through September 30, 2026, not October 31. /faqs-downloads/ already quotes rates 'valid 1 Oct 2026 – 30 Sep 2027', so the two pages overlap and conflict for October 2026. No 2026–27 treatment rate list has been ingested. The INR figures (₹20,495 etc.) do not trace to the reckoner, and the snapshot shows USD/EUR toggles but no USD/EUR tariff values. **Fix:** Needs owner: supply the 2026–27 treatment rate list, correct the validity dates, and verify the INR, USD and EUR figures before publishing.
- [contradiction] “Reservations will be confirmed against 100% advance payments.” — The 2025–26 rate-PDF terms say '40% to secure booking; balance settled at least 30 days prior to admission'. /cancellation-and-refund-policy/ says 100%. The site's sources disagree. **Fix:** Owner ruling on the deposit, then use one wording on every page.
- [contradiction] “All-Inclusive Pricing: No hidden fees! Includes accommodation, meals, therapies, and applicable taxes.” — The same page says internal medicines and tests 'will cost extra', extra treatment is 'As per Doctor’s Prescription', and laundry and extra food are charged. /faqs-downloads/ says packages include 'Herbal medicines and formulations during your stay'. Three positions on whether medicines cost extra. **Fix:** Owner ruling on whether medicines are included. Replace 'No hidden fees!' with an honest list of what is included and what is extra.
- [contradiction] “Transparent Add-Ons: Internal herbal medicines, lab tests, or specialty therapies (e.g., Panchakarma) available as needed.” — The About section describes the core treatment as Nasyam and herbal enemas (Vasti), both Panchakarma procedures. If Panchakarma is an extra charge, the patient cannot tell whether the programme's own therapies are in the nightly rate. **Fix:** Physician/owner to state which respiratory therapies are in the nightly rate and which are prescribed extras.
- [contradiction] “Support: Airport transfers & multilingual staff” — Listed under inclusions, but /faqs-downloads/ lists airport/railway transfers as 'Additional services at extra cost' and quotes separate transfer rates. Which languages 'multilingual' covers is not stated. **Fix:** 'Airport transfers can be arranged at extra cost (see How to Reach).' Name the languages only once the owner confirms them.
- [grammar] “Up to less than 15 days before arrival date or Change in Date during Stay – No Refund.” — Ungrammatical, and the refund windows overlap ('Up to 30 days' and 'Up to 15 days'). 'Cancellation :' also has a space before the colon. **Fix:** '30 or more days before admission: full refund; 15–30 days: 50%; under 15 days, or a date change during your stay: no refund.' This is the wording already on /faqs-downloads/.
- [wrong-fact] “NABH Certified Hospital” — NABH accredits; it does not certify. The awards card on the same page says 'NABH-accredited for Panchakarma', which has a narrower scope than 'Hospital'. **Fix:** 'NABH-accredited'. Owner to confirm the exact scope from certificate AH-2024-0250.
- [typo] “Worlds Luxury Spa Awards Winner 2024” — Typo: 'Worlds' should be 'World' (other cards say 'World Luxury Spa Awards'). The heading 'Worlds Luxury Hotel Awards 2024' has the same problem. **Fix:** 'World Luxury Spa Awards 2024' / 'World Luxury Hotel Awards 2024', subject to the owner's ruling on banned words in award names.
- [locked-word] “Acknowledged for its outstanding service and guest satisfaction” — Banned word 'guest' in Kairali's own descriptive copy (Certificate of Excellence 2018 and 2019 cards), not a proper noun, so no exemption applies. **Fix:** '…for its service and patient satisfaction'.
- [typo] “K.V. Ramesh, Managing Director of Kairali Ayurvedic Group, was honored with the CONSIDICI National Award in 2019.” — The heading spells it 'COSIDICI' and the body spells it 'CONSIDICI'. **Fix:** Use 'COSIDICI' in both places.
- [wrong-fact] “was recognized as the Wellness Brand of the Year – Service Provider in 2016.” — The card's 'Read more' link points to the AYUSH Brand of the Year 2015 article, the same URL as the next card. **Fix:** Point it to the correct 2016 article, or remove the link (owner to supply the URL).
- [unclear] “Manage spondylitis with structured therapies. Support spinal strength and flexibility.” — The related-programmes block was not chosen by content. A spine programme has nothing to do with breathing. The content sibling is /treatment-for-sinusitis-and-migraine/: same rate tier ('Respiratory / ENT / Eye') and it shares Nasyam. **Fix:** Replace the Spondylitis card with Sinus & Migraine Relief. Keep Preventive Health and Panchakarma.

**Must-answer questions missing (10 of 24 gaps):**
- Can I keep taking my inhalers (reliever and inhaled steroid), oral steroids, montelukast, antihistamines or blood thinners during the programme, and will anyone ask me to reduce or stop them? — _The Benefits section promises 'reduced medication dependence', and patients aged 40–70 with asthma or COPD are usually on controller medication. Fear of being told to stop, or of herb–drug interactions, is the first reason they don't book. It is also the biggest safety risk._ Source: needs physician/owner (Dr. Deepu John). /faqs-downloads/ has a drafted but EMPTY slot, 'Can I continue my current (allopathic) medicines during treatment?'
- What happens if I have an asthma attack or severe breathlessness at night? Is a physician on site 24/7, is there oxygen and a nebuliser, and how far is the nearest emergency hospital or ICU? — _Acute exacerbation is the defining fear for this condition, especially for an older patient far from home._ Source: Partly on /faqs-downloads/ ('arrangements with nearby hospitals'; travel insurance with evacuation cover). The 'Is a doctor available at night and on weekends?' slot is EMPTY. Equipment on site and the distance to an ICU need physician/owner.
- Is this programme right for MY diagnosis (COPD/emphysema, bronchiectasis, post-COVID or post-TB lung damage, oxygen-dependent disease), and who do you NOT accept? — _The page names only asthma, bronchitis and allergies and gives no exclusion criteria. Patients with more serious lung disease cannot tell whether they will be turned away on arrival._ Source: needs physician/owner. The /faqs-downloads/ slot 'Which conditions do you NOT treat?' is EMPTY. The free online consultation (/online-consultation/) is the route to a case-by-case answer.
- Are the therapies safe when I get breathless: lying flat for oil therapies, Nasyam in a head-back position, herbal steam, sweating treatments, Vasti? Can they be adapted? — _Steam, heat, strong aromas and lying flat can all trigger or worsen breathlessness. The page lists these therapies without any safety caveat._ Source: needs physician/owner
- How long is the programme really (7, 14, 21 or 28 nights), and what does it cost in my currency? — _The page gives four different durations and shows prices in INR only, with rates valid to 31 Oct 2026. International patients plan flights and leave around the length and the total cost._ Source: The 14-night minimum and the 2025–26 USD/EUR per-night rates (Tier 2: Deluxe $293/€256 single) are in docs/rates/RATES_RECKONER.md but expire 30 Sep 2026. The 2026–27 treatment rate list is missing: needs owner.
- What improvement can I realistically expect, by when, and how will you measure it (spirometry or peak flow before and after)? — _The page swings between 'permanent solution' and 'results vary'. Sceptical patients from Europe, the US and Russia want measurable, honest expectations, and the page gives no timeline._ Source: needs physician/owner. The page says lab tests cost extra but not whether any lung-function tests are done.
- What will the total bill be, including medicines, tests and any extra or Panchakarma therapies? — _'All-inclusive, no hidden fees' sits next to 'medicines and tests will cost extra', while /faqs-downloads/ says medicines are included. A 14-night stay is a large sum, and hidden extras kill trust._ Source: needs owner ruling. The rate-PDF terms, this page and /faqs-downloads/ disagree.
- Who exactly will treat me, what are their qualifications, and do they speak my language (English, German, French, Arabic, Russian)? — _The page says only 'expert Ayurvedic doctors' and names no physician. Language is a safety issue when describing symptoms._ Source: The physician names and BAMS degrees are on /online-consultation/ (Dr. Deepu John, Dr. Akhila Oommen, Dr. Rahul R), and 'Are Ayurvedic doctors real doctors?' is on /faqs-downloads/. Languages need owner: the /faqs-downloads/ slot 'Do the doctors and therapists speak English, German or French?' is EMPTY.
- Will Kerala's heat, humidity, monsoon, pollen, mould or incense/wood smoke make my asthma worse? When is the best season for a respiratory programme? — _Environmental triggers are specific to this condition, and the page is silent on season._ Source: The general climate answer and 'best time for treatments' are on /faqs-downloads/. Respiratory-specific timing and trigger control need physician/owner.
- Should I send my lung-function tests, chest X-ray or CT, and medication list before I travel? Is it safe for me to fly long-haul? — _Patients want to be pre-screened before spending on flights. The page only says to 'bring recent reports'._ Source: The pre-programme teleconsultation is on this page and the free consultation on /online-consultation/. The /faqs-downloads/ slot 'Should I send my medical reports before I come?' is EMPTY. Fitness to fly needs physician/owner.

**For Dr. Deepu John:** Cure-style promises in the About section: 'Seeking a permanent solution for bronchial issues?', 'treatment package to eliminate bronchial diseases', 'Say goodbye to bronchial issues'. These need removal or physician-approved replacement wording. · Benefit claim 'reduced medication dependence'. Highest risk: it implies asthma or COPD patients can reduce inhalers or steroids. Needs removal, plus an explicit 'do not stop prescribed medicines' statement. · Benefit claims 'minimized relapses', 'improved lung function, and reduced inflammation', 'detoxification of the respiratory system', 'Strengthened immunity'. · Approach section: 'Ayurvedic remedies… enhance lung function, reduce inflammation, and boost immunity'; 'ensuring lasting relief and optimal respiratory health'; 'to prevent future occurrences'; 'Rely on our… successful outcomes'. · 'These therapies address the root causes of bronchial diseases and lead to significant improvements in patients’ health' (unqualified outcome). · 'we’ve aided 30,000+ patients… to treat bronchial conditions': an outcome volume claim with no source, which may be read as 30,000 bronchial patients. · Hero line 'to improve breathing and boost immunity'. · 'Who is this for' includes asthma patients with no exclusion or contraindication criteria (acute severe asthma, oxygen dependence, active infection, pregnancy, cardiac disease). · Process step 03: the physician 'will diagnose the specific type… such as asthma, bronchitis, or respiratory allergies'. What diagnostic basis (spirometry, imaging, reports)? · Process step 05 names 'nasal irrigation' while About names Nasyam and herbal enemas (Vasti). The physician must confirm which respiratory therapies are actually used and that they are safe for asthmatic patients (steam, oils, head position). · Process step 06: herbal formulations to 'reduce inflammation, strengthen the immune system, and improve lung function'. · Process step 08: 'Once the symptoms subside' presumes symptoms resolve. · Post-treatment: 'Ongoing support ensures long-term health'. During treatment: 'for a safe and effective journey'. · FAQ 1 comparative claim: 'Unlike conventional medicine… rather than solely treating symptoms'. Needs an 'alongside, not instead of' statement. · FAQ 2 answer: 'can provide long-term relief and significantly improve the quality of life'. The question itself needs a plain physician answer on whether a chronic condition can be fully resolved. · FAQ 3 answer: 'generally safe when administered by qualified practitioners'. Needs physician-written contraindications, herb–drug interaction and emergency content. · FAQ 4 duration 'a week to four weeks' vs the 14-night minimum in the rate list and tariff. The physician/owner must fix the clinically appropriate length. · Testimonials making outcome claims ('My lungs started to open up… congestion and wheezing started to go away'; 'helped me in ways that traditional therapies were never able to'). Provenance and consent need verification, and the medical claims need approval. · Daily routine prescribes 30–45 minutes of asana, plus pranayama and yoga twice daily, for every patient. The physician must confirm suitability for breathless patients or label it as adapted per patient. · Package note makes Panchakarma an add-on while the About copy describes Nasyam and Vasti as core. The physician/owner must define which therapies are in the programme's standard protocol.

### /beauty-care-and-eye-care/ — weak

Eye health programme ("Vision Health & Eye Rejuvenation Therapy", Netram Chikitsa). The page promises better vision and relief from eye strain, dryness and "various eye conditions". It lists Netra Tarpana/Tharpanam, Netra Basti, Nasyam and Pindi, plus herbal medicines, eye drops, diet and yoga, in a residential programme of 7, 14, 21 or 28 nights with a 7-night minimum. The page never names a single diagnosis it treats or excludes: not glaucoma, cataract, macular degeneration, diabetic retinopathy, dry eye disease or presbyopia. It is thin on clinical detail (4 generic FAQs, a daily routine that is not specific to eyes) and heavy on hospitality wording. By content this is a head/eye (Shalakya) programme. Its only shared therapy with a sibling page is Nasyam, used on /treatment-for-sinusitis-and-migraine/. It shares nothing with the skin/psoriasis pages it sits next to in the current "Skin & eyes" sitemap group, so that grouping looks like it comes from the legacy "beauty-care" slug, not from the content.

FAQ questions on the page: 4.

**Proofreading (verified):**
- [contradiction] “Ayurvedic eye care complements conventional treatments, eases symptoms, aids healing, and may reduce medication needs.” — This contradicts FAQ 4, which says to consult your healthcare provider before changing any medication. It also invites a glaucoma patient to cut their eye-pressure drops, and stopping those can cause permanent sight loss. **Fix:** Remove 'and may reduce medication needs'. Replace with a sentence that treatment runs alongside, never instead of, what the patient's eye specialist prescribes (wording for Dr. Deepu John to approve).
- [overclaim-medical] “Ayurvedic therapies and medications are derived from natural sources, offering safety and minimal side effects compared to conventional treatments.” — Claims 'natural' means safe and makes an unsupported safety comparison with conventional eye care. Medicated ghee and oil put into the eye, and Nasya, carry real risks such as irritation and infection. **Fix:** Remove the comparison. Say therapies are prescribed and supervised by the physician after reviewing the patient's eye history (Dr. Deepu John to word).
- [overclaim-medical] “Ayurvedic treatments also balance doshas for overall well-being, increasing focus and concentration, and preventing age-related eye conditions.” — 'Preventing age-related eye conditions' implies protection from cataract and macular degeneration. That is an unsupported prevention claim aimed at exactly the 40–70 audience. **Fix:** Remove 'preventing age-related eye conditions', or replace it with physician-approved supportive wording.
- [overclaim-medical] “So, it needs to be addressed for deep-rooted and everlasting results.” — Promises permanent results. That breaks the no-outcome-promise rule and contradicts FAQ 3 ('results vary'). **Fix:** Remove 'everlasting'. For example: 'so it deserves early, careful attention.'
- [overclaim-medical] “offering you an exclusive chance to experience the world with a new vision with a 7/14/21/28 natural eye care program based on your condition” — Implies restored vision. 'Exclusive chance' is sales hype. '7/14/21/28' reads as a code, and 'program' breaks the programme spelling used elsewhere. **Fix:** '…offers a residential eye-care programme of 7, 14, 21 or 28 nights; your physician recommends the length at consultation.'
- [contradiction] “a 7/14/21/28 natural eye care program based on your condition” — /faqs-downloads/ says programmes 'range from a 3-night preventive stay to 21-night programmes' and its stay-length table does not list an eye programme, while this page offers 28 nights. One of the two pages is wrong. **Fix:** Owner to confirm whether a 28-night eye programme exists, then make this page and /faqs-downloads/ agree.
- [overclaim-medical] “The eye care routine will resuscitate them and revive their scintillating spark with Netra Tharpanam, Pindi and other therapies, depending on the need.” — 'Resuscitate' is the wrong word (it means reviving someone from cardiac arrest) and overstates the effect. 'Pindi' is never explained. **Fix:** 'Therapies such as Netra Tarpana and Pindi (a herbal poultice over closed eyes — physician to confirm description) are chosen according to your assessment.'
- [grammar] “including herbal juices to atone you internally” — Wrong word: 'atone' means to make amends. 'Tone' or 'nourish' was probably meant. **Fix:** 'including herbal juices that support you from within'
- [unclear] “Yoga classes will also be given to return to the land of the living and provide full energy to the body.” — Wrong idiom: it implies patients arrive half-dead. Inappropriate on a hospital page. **Fix:** 'Daily yoga and breathing practice support rest and circulation.'
- [unclear] “Vision is important only when one starts losing it.” — Says the opposite of what was meant and is insensitive to readers who are already losing sight. **Fix:** 'Most of us only value our eyesight when it starts to change.'
- [unclear] “Eyes can add a lot to your beauty, though we all tend to misuse them in the age of computers, tablets, smartphones, LED TVs etc.” — Cosmetic framing on a clinical eye programme, probably inherited from the legacy 'beauty-care' slug. It also explains the wrong sitemap grouping ('Skin & eyes' next to psoriasis). By content this programme belongs with the head/eye therapies. Its only shared therapy with a sibling page is Nasyam on /treatment-for-sinusitis-and-migraine/. **Fix:** Rewrite the opening around screen strain, dryness and age-related change; keep the URL. In the sitemap, group it by content (eye/head care), not by the slug.
- [unclear] “Netra Tarpanam (eye rejuvenation therapy), and Netra Basti (medicated eye baths).” — Presents Tarpana and Basti as two different therapies. The two names are often used for the same dough-ring medicated-ghee procedure, and 'eye baths' may describe a different procedure. The page also spells the therapies inconsistently: Netra Tarpana, Netra Tharpanam, Netra Tarpanam, Netrabasti, Netra Basti, Netram Chikitsa. **Fix:** Dr. Deepu John to confirm which procedures are actually given and write a one-line description of each. Then use one spelling of each name throughout.
- [grammar] “Diet, lifestyle, and herbal meds play crucial roles.” — 'Meds' is too informal for the clinical register. **Fix:** 'Diet, lifestyle and herbal medicines play a central role.'
- [locked-word] “Our approach empowers clients to manage their eye health long-term. At our retreats, we provide compassionate, comprehensive Ayurvedic eye care” — Uses 'clients' and 'retreats' instead of the required clinical register. 'Retreats' (plural) also misdescribes a single hospital campus. **Fix:** '…helps patients manage their eye health long-term. At the hospital, we provide…'
- [locked-word] “Trust in Ayurveda and our commitment to your eye care journey.” — Banned word 'journey' (KTAHV lock). **Fix:** '…and our commitment to your eye care.'
- [locked-word] “with continued doctor support for a safe and effective journey.” — Banned word 'journey'. **Fix:** '…with your physician reviewing your progress throughout your admission.'
- [locked-word] “Personalized Care: Daily wellness check-ins with your Ayurvedic doctor to tailor your journey.” — Banned word 'journey'. **Fix:** 'Daily physician review to adjust your treatment.'
- [locked-word] “Inclusions — Your Holistic Wellness Journey Includes” — Banned word 'Journey'. **Fix:** 'What your programme includes'
- [locked-word] “Explore our certified organic farm where medicinal herbs and vegetables are grown using traditional sustainable methods. Learn about the journey from seed to medicine.” — Banned word 'journey'. The 'certified organic' claim names no certifying body anywhere on the page. **Fix:** '…how a medicinal plant becomes a medicine.' Owner to confirm the certification or drop 'certified'.
- [locked-word] “World Luxury Award Winner” — Banned word 'Luxury'. This is a Kairali-written trust chip (shown twice, including the closing strip), not an award's proper name. **Fix:** Replace with 'Internationally awarded', or a clinical proof point such as 'NABH-accredited'.
- [locked-word] “Luxury, vastu-compliant villas with modern amenities” — Banned word 'Luxury'. **Fix:** 'Private, vastu-compliant villas for inpatient stay'
- [locked-word] “Luxury Stay: Unwind in private, eco-friendly villas designed for tranquility, blending traditional charm with modern comforts.” — Banned word 'Luxury' plus hotel-style wording. **Fix:** 'Accommodation: a private villa for the length of your admission.'
- [locked-word] “Personalized therapies, including massages and herbal treatments, will be administered.” — 'Massages' is spa register on a hospital page, and names none of the eye therapies. **Fix:** 'Your prescribed therapies — for example Netra Tarpana and Nasyam — are given daily.'
- [locked-word] “Healing Eyes, Naturally: Client Story of Hope and Clarity” — 'Client' instead of 'patient'. **Fix:** 'A patient's experience'
- [locked-word] “socializing with fellow retreat participants” — 'Retreat participants' is hospitality register. **Fix:** '…with fellow patients'
- [locked-word] “Check-in: 12:00 noon” — Check-in/check-out is hotel wording. The required register is admission/discharge. **Fix:** 'Admission: from 12:00 noon · Discharge: by 10:00 AM'
- [locked-word] “Acknowledged for its outstanding service and guest satisfaction” — Banned word 'guest' in Kairali-written blurb text (shown twice: the 2018 and 2019 certificates). This is not an award's proper name. **Fix:** '…for outstanding service and patient satisfaction'. Owner to rule on award proper names such as 'World Luxury Spa Awards' (flagged, not counted, per DECISIONS.md).
- [locked-word] “Celebrated as a leading wellness and spa brand” — Banned word 'spa' in Kairali-written blurb text, not an award name. **Fix:** 'Recognised at the South Asian Travel Awards 2018.'
- [contradiction] “I could feel the impact almost immediately. My vision is clearer, and I experience less strain even after long work hours.” — This single testimonial ('almost immediately', 'vision is clearer') contradicts FAQ 3 ('improvements in a few weeks'). It sits under the heading 'Transformative Results'. **Fix:** Keep only if it is verified and consented. Retitle the section 'A patient's experience' and add 'individual experiences vary'.
- [contradiction] “Engage in an Ayurvedic therapy session, such as Abhyanga (Ayurvedic massage) or Udwarthana (herbal powder massage), to promote detoxification and relaxation.” — The routine is generic across programmes. It names no eye therapy, Udwarthana is a metabolic/weight therapy, and it shows one therapy slot while the package promises two a day. **Fix:** Replace with the eye programme's actual daily schedule, including when Netra Tarpana/Nasyam are given (physician to supply).
- [contradiction] “All-Inclusive Pricing: No hidden fees! Includes accommodation, meals, therapies, and applicable taxes.” — Contradicts the same page's terms: 'Internal Medicines and test reports… can only be ascertained as your treatment progresses and will cost extra'. Panchakarma, extra treatment, laundry and internet are also charged. **Fix:** 'Programme rate includes accommodation, meals, two daily therapies and taxes. Medicines, tests and additional therapies are charged as prescribed.'
- [contradiction] “The above rates are valid from April 1, 2025, to October 31, 2026.” — /faqs-downloads/ gives 'current rates (valid 1 Oct 2026 – 30 Sep 2027)'. The two periods overlap, and a patient booking for November 2026 or later has no valid price on this page. **Fix:** Owner to confirm which rate period applies. Update this page, and every other page carrying the same tariff block, to one set of dates.
- [contradiction] “Support: Airport transfers & multilingual staff” — Reads as if transfers are included. /faqs-downloads/ lists them as paid (for example ₹6,775 one way from Coimbatore Airport). The languages spoken are not named. **Fix:** 'Airport and station transfers arranged at extra cost (see How to Reach).' Owner to name the languages the staff actually speak.
- [grammar] “Up to less than 15 days before arrival date or Change in Date during Stay – No Refund.” — Garbled. The bands above it ('Up to 30 days… Full Refund', 'Up to 15 days… 50%') are ambiguous about which days they cover. **Fix:** Align with /faqs-downloads/: '30 or more days before admission: full refund; 15–29 days: 50%; fewer than 15 days, or a date change during the stay: no refund'. Owner to confirm the day-30 boundary.
- [unclear] “(Note: Preventive Health & Regenerative Program includes one therapy per day.)” — Boilerplate about a different programme, confusing on the eye page. **Fix:** Remove it from this page.
- [unclear] “Terms & Conditions — This package will also include” — The heading promises inclusions, but the list below is terms and charges. **Fix:** 'Terms & conditions'
- [unclear] “The above rates are inclusive of applicable taxes*.” — The asterisk has no footnote, and the statement is repeated later ('All rates and packages are inclusive of all taxes.'). **Fix:** Drop the asterisk and keep one tax statement.
- [grammar] “Abstinence from Smoking, Alcohol, & Coffee is strictly adhered to.” — Awkward wording and random capitals. **Fix:** 'Smoking, alcohol and coffee are not permitted during treatment.'
- [grammar] “For children below 5 years, stay and meals are Complimentary.” — Stray capital, 'Child Policy :' has a space before the colon, and the 5–12 rule given on /faqs-downloads/ is missing. **Fix:** 'Children under 5 stay and eat free; ages 5–12 are charged 50% for food and extra bedding (per /faqs-downloads/).'
- [grammar] “*Doctor might change as subject to availability.*” — The Markdown asterisks show as literal characters, the grammar is broken, and the line worries patients about continuity of care. **Fix:** 'Your assigned physician may change, subject to availability.'
- [wrong-fact] “WHO says around 80% of the world’s population is estimated to use traditional medicine. Are you one of 80% of people?” — No source is given. The widely repeated WHO '80%' figure is usually cited for particular regions, not the whole world. The grammar is also off ('one of 80% of people'). **Fix:** Cite the exact WHO source or remove the sentence. If kept: 'Are you one of them?'
- [unclear] “Do not waste valuable time; nothing is more important than you and the health of your loved ones.” — Fear-based pressure line. It sits badly with a clinical, low-pressure call to action. **Fix:** 'Questions before you decide? Our physicians answer them in a free consultation.'
- [grammar] “Eye rejuvenation Ayurvedic therapy are generally safe when administered by experienced practitioners.” — Subject–verb disagreement ('therapy are'). **Fix:** 'Ayurvedic eye therapies are generally safe when…' (safety wording itself needs physician sign-off)
- [unclear] “Ayurvedic treatment for eye health can be beneficial for a wide range of eye conditions, but the effectiveness may vary” — Dodges the question: it names no condition treated and no condition excluded. **Fix:** List the conditions the physician accepts and the ones referred elsewhere (Dr. Deepu John to supply).
- [unclear] “Traditional eye care therapy in India can complement conventional medicine, but it’s important to consult with your primary healthcare provider” — Never says a clear 'no'. It points to a primary care doctor rather than the prescribing eye specialist, and promises coordination with 'your medical team' with no stated process. **Fix:** Start with 'No — continue all prescribed eye drops and medicines unless your eye specialist changes them.' Owner to confirm what coordination is actually offered.
- [wrong-fact] “NABH Certified Hospital” — NABH accredits rather than certifies, and the page's own awards block states the scope as 'NABH-accredited for Panchakarma'. **Fix:** 'NABH-accredited (Panchakarma)'
- [grammar] “Our Approach Vision Health & Eye Rejuvenation Therapy” — Missing preposition. **Fix:** 'Our approach to vision health and eye rejuvenation'
- [typo] “Why Vision Health & Eye Rejuvenation Therapy ?” — Stray space before the question mark. **Fix:** 'Why this programme?'
- [typo] “Netram Chikitsa ( Kairali’s Treatment for Eye Care )” — Spaces inside the parentheses. The og:title in route-manifest also runs the two lines together: 'Vision Health & Eye Rejuvenation TherapyNetram Chikitsa ( … )'. **Fix:** 'Netram Chikitsa (Kairali's treatment for eye care)'; add a separator in og:title.
- [typo] “MORE PROGRAMs” — Casing error. The page also mixes 'Program' ('Who is This Program For?', 'Personalized Doctor-Guided Program') with 'Programme'. **Fix:** 'More programmes'; use 'programme' throughout.
- [typo] “Deep detox with revitalizing therapies.Restore strength, clarity, and vitality.” — Missing space after the full stop. **Fix:** '…therapies. Restore…'
- [unclear] “History” — The on-page nav link 'History' jumps to the 1908 / 'Our Approach' section. The page has no history content. **Fix:** Rename the link 'Our approach', or point it to real content.
- [typo] “Worlds Luxury Spa Awards Winner 2024” — 'Worlds' should be 'World' (same error in 'Worlds Luxury Hotel Awards 2024'). **Fix:** 'World Luxury Spa Awards 2024' (owner to rule on showing award names that contain banned words on KTAHV pages).
- [wrong-fact] “Mr. Abhilash K. Ramesh was honored as one of the 40 influential leaders in the wellness industry in 2018.” — The linked article slug reads 'most-influential-leaders-under-40', which suggests an 'under 40' list rather than '40 leaders'. The page names the award 'COSIDICI' in one heading and 'CONSIDICI' in the body of that entry, and the 'Wellness Brand of the Year 2016' card's Read more links to the AYUSH 2015 article. **Fix:** Check each against its source article and correct it (this is the shared awards block, so fix it once).
- [overclaim-medical] “Improve vision & relieve eye strain with Ayurvedic eye care therapies at Kairali.” — 'Improve vision' in the hero line and meta description is an unqualified outcome promise. **Fix:** 'Ayurvedic care for tired, strained and dry eyes, planned by a physician.' (Dr. Deepu John to approve)

**Must-answer questions missing (9 of 25 gaps):**
- Which eye conditions do you actually treat, and which do you not? For example glaucoma, cataract, macular degeneration, diabetic retinopathy, dry eye, screen strain, presbyopia. — _The page only says 'Individuals with various eye conditions'. A 60-year-old with AMD or glaucoma cannot tell whether to fly 8+ hours, and without a named condition there is no reason to book._ Source: needs physician/owner (Dr. Deepu John: indications and exclusions list). /all-programs/ only says 'For: eye care, eyesight'.
- I use glaucoma drops or other prescription eye drops. Must I stop them, and are your Ayurvedic eye drops, Netra Tarpana and Nasyam safe alongside them? — _The page says treatment 'may reduce medication needs' and mentions prescribed 'eye drops'. The most common fear for this group is interactions, or being told to stop sight-saving drops._ Source: needs physician/owner
- Can this reverse my cataract or let me put off cataract surgery? — _The most common hope among 60+ patients, and 'improved vision clarity' / 'a new vision' invite it. An honest answer protects the patient and Kairali from an overclaim._ Source: needs physician/owner
- I've had cataract surgery, LASIK or retinal surgery, or I get eye injections for macular degeneration. Is Netra Tarpana safe for me, and how long after surgery must I wait? — _Many 40–70 patients have had eye procedures. Putting medicated ghee into the eye after surgery is an obvious safety worry the page never mentions._ Source: needs physician/owner
- Will you measure my eyesight and eye pressure at admission and discharge? Is an ophthalmologist or optometrist involved? — _Without an objective baseline the patient cannot tell whether it worked. The page only mentions an Ayurvedic 'diagnosis of underlying imbalances', and 'lab tests' are charged extra._ Source: needs physician/owner
- What change can I realistically expect by the end of 7, 14, 21 or 28 nights, and how is my length of stay decided? — _FAQ 3 says 'a few weeks', the testimonial says 'almost immediately', and the programme can be 7 nights. The patient cannot choose a duration or budget for it._ Source: needs physician/owner. The duration options and 7-night minimum are on this page; /faqs-downloads/ contradicts the 28-night option.
- What will the whole stay cost, and are Netra Tarpana and Nasyam among the two included daily therapies, or extra like medicines and tests? — _The page says 'All-Inclusive… No hidden fees' but also that medicines and tests 'will cost extra' and are only known 'as your treatment progresses'. Nasya is a Panchakarma procedure, and 'Panchakarma' is listed as an add-on._ Source: This page (per-night rates, 7-night minimum, add-on prices) plus /all-programs/ ('From ₹1,43,465' for 7 nights). Whether eye therapies are included, and a typical medicine/test range: needs physician/owner.
- Who will treat me? What are their qualifications and eye-specific (Shalakya Tantra) experience, will the same physician see me throughout, and do they speak my language (German, French, Russian, Arabic)? — _The page names no physician, says 'Doctor might change as subject to availability', and claims 'multilingual staff' without naming languages. Trust depends on the treating doctor._ Source: /online-consultation/ (Dr. Deepu John, Chief Physician & Head of Department, BAMS; Dr. Akhila Oommen, BAMS; Dr. Rahul R, BAMS) and /faqs-downloads/ ('Are Ayurvedic doctors real doctors?'). Eye specialisation and languages spoken: needs physician/owner.
- What if it doesn't help, or I must leave early for a medical reason? Do I get anything back? — _Payment is 100% in advance and 'Change in Date during Stay – No Refund'. For a costly international trip with an uncertain outcome, this is the booking blocker._ Source: This page (100% advance, cancellation terms) plus /cancellation-and-refund-policy/. The early-discharge / medical-reason policy: needs physician/owner.

**For Dr. Deepu John:** Hero line / meta description: 'Improve vision & relieve eye strain with Ayurvedic eye care therapies at Kairali.' This is an unqualified vision-improvement promise. · About: 'it needs to be addressed for deep-rooted and everlasting results' and 'experience the world with a new vision'. These promise permanent results and restored vision. · About: 'The eye care routine will resuscitate them and revive their scintillating spark'. This overstates the effect of the therapies. · Our Approach: 'Our therapies like Nasyam, Netra Tarpanam, and Netrabasti cleanse, nourish, and improve circulation in the eyes, enhancing vision and reducing strain'. Both the mechanism and the efficacy claims need sign-off. · Our Approach: 'Together, we unlock vibrant and healthy vision for renewed clarity and joy.' This is an outcome promise. · Why section: 'Ayurvedic therapies and medications are derived from natural sources, offering safety and minimal side effects compared to conventional treatments.' This is a comparative safety claim. · Why section: 'complements conventional treatments, eases symptoms, aids healing, and may reduce medication needs'. It could lead a glaucoma patient to stop eye-pressure drops, and it contradicts FAQ 4. HIGHEST RISK. · Why section: 'addressing the root cause of eye conditions… for long-lasting results'. This is a permanence claim. · Benefits: 'improved vision clarity', 'alleviates dryness, discomfort, and inflammation', 'strengthening ocular tissues', 'increasing focus and concentration' and 'preventing age-related eye conditions'. The prevention claim is the most serious. · Process step 5: 'Herbal medications and eye drops may be prescribed to nourish the eyes and promote healing'. Needs sign-off on which eye drops, their sterility, and their use alongside existing prescriptions. · Process step 4: calling Netra Tarpanam and Netra Basti two separate therapies ('eye rejuvenation therapy' vs 'medicated eye baths'). The physician needs to confirm what is actually given and how each is described. · Who is this programme for: 'Individuals with various eye conditions'. Needs a physician-approved list of indications and exclusions (glaucoma, cataract, AMD, diabetic retinopathy, recent eye surgery, active infection). · FAQ 1: 'can be beneficial for a wide range of eye conditions'. An efficacy claim with no conditions named. · FAQ 2: 'Eye rejuvenation Ayurvedic therapy are generally safe… They use natural ingredients'. Presents 'natural' as the basis for safety and omits infection risk, contact lenses and post-surgery cases. · FAQ 3: 'Some individuals may experience improvements in a few weeks'. The timeline needs to match the 7–28-night programme and what happens after discharge. · FAQ 4: 'Can Ayurvedic eye care treatment replace my prescription medications?' The answer must say clearly 'No — continue prescribed eye medication', and the offer to coordinate with 'your medical team' must be a real service. · Testimonial under 'Transformative Results': 'I could feel the impact almost immediately. My vision is clearer'. An outcome testimonial that needs verification and patient consent, plus an 'individual results vary' qualifier. · FAQ intro: 'WHO says around 80% of the world's population is estimated to use traditional medicine'. A health statistic with no source that is commonly misquoted. · Daily routine: therapy slot names Abhyanga/Udwarthana 'to promote detoxification'. The physician needs to confirm which therapies the eye programme actually uses. · 'Continuous post-discharge wellness plan' / 'Ongoing support ensures long-term health'. These are continuity-of-care promises; the physician should confirm what follow-up is actually provided (/faqs-downloads/ says online follow-up at 1, 3 and 6 months).

### /treatment-to-remove-stress-and-strain/ — weak

Stress and mental strain, plus the symptoms that come with them (anxiety, tension, poor sleep, low energy, muscle tension). The page calls it 'Manas Shānthi Chikitsa', a residential Ayurvedic programme of 7/14/21/28 days, minimum 7 nights. Note for the sitemap flow: in content, this page's closest siblings are /sleep-restoration-therapy/ (same villa tariff; its hero also claims to relieve 'insomnia, anxiety & stress') and /yoga-based-stress-energy-reset-program/. Its 'Discover More' block does not link to either one. It links Weight Management, Panchakarma and Rejuvenation instead.

FAQ questions on the page: 1.

**Proofreading (verified):**
- [overclaim-medical] “Ayurvedic medications are generally considered safe when prescribed by a qualified Ayurvedic doctor. They are derived from natural sources and have minimal side effects.” — This is the only FAQ, and it overclaims. It treats 'natural' as meaning 'safe' and promises 'minimal side effects'. It says nothing about interactions with the drugs stress patients often take (antidepressants, anti-anxiety drugs, sleeping pills, blood-pressure drugs, blood thinners) or about medicine quality. The FAQPage JSON-LD repeats the same text, so the overclaim also goes into search results. **Fix:** Dr. Deepu John to write the replacement answer. It should say that the treating physician reviews your full medication list at consultation, that you must declare every prescription, and that you never stop a prescribed medicine without your own doctor (wording already used on /online-consultation/). Any side-effect statement comes from the physician. Update the FAQPage JSON-LD in the same edit.
- [locked-word] “Here are the questions guests most often ask before starting the stress relief & mental wellness programme.” — Uses the banned word 'guests'. It also promises 'the questions' (plural) but only one question follows. **Fix:** 'Questions patients ask before admission to the Stress Relief & Mental Wellness programme.' Then add the missing patient questions (see the objection gaps).
- [contradiction] “With two decades of experience, we’ve treated individuals struggling with stress and strain using Ayurvedic therapies.” — Contradicts the About section two blocks above ('With over 118 years of experience') and the '118+ Years Heritage' trust strip. The page gives two different experience figures. **Fix:** Owner to choose one true statement, for example the family practice since 1908, plus the year the Healing Village began treating stress conditions (needs owner). Use it in both sections.
- [overclaim-medical] “with safety ensured through natural sources for medications” — Medical overclaim: being natural does not make a medicine safe. Safety comes from assessment, dosing and monitoring. **Fix:** Delete it, or replace with physician-approved wording such as 'every medicine is prescribed and monitored by your treating physician'.
- [overclaim-medical] “Our clients have experienced profound transformations in physical and emotional well-being.” — Unsupported outcome claim on a mental-health (YMYL) page. 'Clients' is outside the required patient register. **Fix:** Remove it, or replace with a verifiable, physician-approved statement. Use 'patients'.
- [overclaim-medical] “We prioritize trust, understanding, and personalized care to lead you to a stress-free life.” — Promises an outcome ('a stress-free life'). The History section repeats it ('the path to a balanced, stress-free life'). **Fix:** '…to help you manage stress with a plan you can continue at home.' Physician to approve.
- [overclaim-medical] “Reduces levels of anxiety and tension. Increases focus and mental clarity as well as better sleep quality.” — States outcomes as certain. The grammar is broken: 'Increases … as well as better sleep quality'. **Fix:** 'Aims to reduce tension and support better focus and sleep.' Wording and any claim level need physician sign-off.
- [grammar] “Improves digestion, more vitality, and boosts immunity.” — The list items don't match grammatically. 'Boosts immunity' is an unsupported medical claim. **Fix:** 'Supports digestion and energy.' Drop 'boosts immunity' unless the physician approves it.
- [locked-word] “From Tension to Tranquility: Guest Journeys to Stress-Free Living” — Two banned words ('Guest', 'Journeys'). The same word appears in 'Luxury, vastu-compliant villas with modern amenities' (Inclusions card) and 'Premium luxury experience' (Royal Villa tariff card). **Fix:** 'Accommodation: private, eco-friendly villas…'. Also change the Inclusions card to 'Vastu-compliant villas with modern amenities' and the Royal Villa line to 'Larger villa with additional space'.

### /treatment-for-hypertension/ — weak

Hypertension (high blood pressure), framed as "Cardiovascular Health & Blood Pressure Treatment" (Rakta Samadama Chikitsa). It is an inpatient Ayurvedic programme of 14/21/28 days with a 14-night minimum, from ₹3,01,938 for 14 nights (Deluxe single, per /all-programs/). The hero and meta also promise "heart health".

FAQ questions on the page: 4.

**Proofreading (verified):**
- [locked-word] “Can Ayurvedic treatment completely cure hypertension?” — FAQ question uses the banned word 'cure'. Its framing also invites the reader to expect that a complete cure is possible. **Fix:** Reframe the question and have the physician answer it, e.g. 'Can this programme replace my blood-pressure medication?' or 'What can this programme realistically do for my blood pressure?'. Remove 'cure' in all locales.
- [overclaim-medical] “While it can effectively manage hypertension, a complete cure depends on various factors and individual responses.” — Banned word 'cure' again. The sentence implies a complete cure happens for some patients, and 'effectively manage' is an unqualified efficacy claim. **Fix:** Rewrite with Dr. Deepu John: say the programme aims to support blood-pressure control alongside the patient's prescribed care. Do not say or imply cure.
- [contradiction] “Ayurvedic treatment can be used alongside conventional medications for hypertension.” — Blanket safety statement about combining treatment with antihypertensives. The site's own FAQ ledger (faq-content-2026.json, 'Can I continue my current (allopathic) medicines during treatment?') marks this policy as unconfirmed, pending Dr. Deepu John. It also contradicts the page's promise of 'reducing reliance on synthetic drugs'. **Fix:** Hold until Dr. Deepu John supplies the policy. Suggested content: never stop or change BP medicines without your prescribing doctor; our physicians record your medicines at admission and monitor BP daily; any adjustment is coordinated with your own doctor.
- [overclaim-medical] “Ayurvedic therapies and herbal medicines provide natural alternatives, reducing reliance on synthetic drugs and their potential side effects.” — Presents Ayurveda as an alternative to antihypertensives. This can encourage patients to stop medication, and it contradicts the FAQ, which says to use the treatment alongside conventional medicines. The Benefits line 'reducing reliance on synthetic medications' and the audience line 'seeking natural alternatives' repeat the same idea. **Fix:** Remove or rewrite as complementary care that works with the patient's existing treatment, worded by the physician.
- [contradiction] “While some clients experience improvements within a few weeks, it generally takes several months of consistent treatment to achieve significant and sustainable results.” — Contradicts the page's 14/21/28-day stay and the testimonial ('by the time I left, my blood pressure had notably returned to normal'). It also never says what the 'several months' after discharge consist of. 'clients' is off-register. **Fix:** Use 'patients'. Have the physician state what can be expected by discharge versus after it, and link the post-discharge plan (home medicines, follow-ups at 1/3/6 months per /faqs-downloads/).

Who decides, and how do you stop my pressure dropping too low once herbs and therapies are added? — _This is the first safety question for any patient on antihypertensives, and it decides whether their own cardiologist or GP objects. Dr. Deepu John must answer. The site's own FAQ ledger (faq-content-2026.json, 'Can I continue my current (allopathic) medicines during treatment?') is marked no-recorded-fact.
- I also have diabetes, high cholesterol, kidney disease, or a past heart attack, stent, stroke or atrial fibrillation. Am I eligible? Is there a blood-pressure level or heart condition at which you would not admit me? — _At 40–70, hypertension rarely comes alone. The title says 'Cardiovascular Health' and the hero 'heart health', which suggests cardiac patients are covered, but no eligibility or exclusion is stated._ Source: needs physician/owner. The /faqs-downloads/ ledger items 'Which conditions do you NOT treat?' and 'Can I take Panchakarma if I have diabetes or high blood pressure?' are both unanswered, pending Dr. Deepu John.
- Are the heat and oil treatments safe with high blood pressure (herbal steam baths, Abhyangam, Shirodhara, Udwarthana)? Is my BP checked before each therapy, and what happens if it is high that morning? — _Inclusions promise daily 'herbal steam baths' and the routine names Udwarthana. This generic copy is shared with other programmes, and a hypertensive reader worries about spikes._ Source: needs physician/owner: therapy selection for hypertension and the pre-therapy BP-check protocol.
- What can I realistically expect by discharge after 14 nights, and how will it be measured (daily BP charting; lipids, kidney function and ECG at admission and discharge)? Will I get the readings in writing? — _The FAQ says results take 'several months', the stay is 14–28 days, and the testimonial says BP 'returned to normal' by departure. The patient needs honest expectations and objective measures to justify a long-haul trip._ Source: The expected outcome and monitoring protocol need physician/owner. /faqs-downloads/ ('Will I receive a discharge summary...') confirms admission and discharge notes, treatment summaries and prescriptions are provided.
- What will the total cost be? The rates are per night, but herbal medicines and lab tests are extra and 'can only be ascertained as your treatment progresses'. What is the typical range for this programme, and when do I pay it? — _A 100% advance plus an open-ended medicine bill contradicts 'All-Inclusive Pricing: No hidden fees!'. International patients budget in EUR/USD and need a ceiling._ Source: The 14-night total (from ₹3,01,938, Deluxe single) is on /all-programs/ and could be shown here. The typical medicine and lab cost range needs physician/owner.
- Who exactly will treat me (names, qualifications, experience)? Do they speak English, German, French, Russian or Arabic? Is a modern-medicine doctor or cardiologist available if needed? — _This page says only 'Ayurvedic doctor' and 'Doctor might change as subject to availability', and claims 'multilingual staff' without naming languages._ Source: Qualifications are on /online-consultation/: Dr. Deepu John, Chief Physician & HOD, BAMS; Dr. Akhila Oommen, BAMS; Dr. Rahul R, BAMS. Languages need physician/owner (FAQ ledger: no-recorded-fact). Allopathic or cardiology cover needs physician/owner.
- If I have a pressure spike, chest pain or stroke symptoms at night, who responds, and how far is the nearest hospital with a cardiac unit? — _This is the core fear of a hypertensive patient flying to rural Kerala, and the page does not address it._ Source: Partly on /faqs-downloads/ ('arrangements with nearby hospitals and emergency services'; recommends travel insurance with evacuation cover). Night and weekend physician cover, and the named cardiac hospital with travel time, need physician/owner (ledger item 'Is a doctor available at night and on weekends?' is unanswered).
- Do I need a medical visa? Will you send an invitation letter? Do I get my 100% advance back if the visa is refused? — _The T&C require full prepayment, and visa refusal is a real risk for some nationalities (Middle East, Russia)._ Source: /faqs-downloads/ covers e-visa vs Medical Visa (points to indianvisaonline.gov.in) and says an invitation letter is available on request. The refund on visa refusal needs physician/owner (ledger item 'Is the advance payment refundable if my visa is refused?' is unanswered).

The site's own FAQ ledger marks the allopathic-medicine policy as unconfirmed, pending Dr. Deepu John. It needs his wording on continuing, adjusting and monitoring antihypertensives, including hypotension risk. · Medication-replacement messaging that could lead patients to stop BP medicines: 'Ayurvedic therapies and herbal medicines provide natural alternatives, reducing reliance on synthetic drugs and their potential side effects'; Benefits 'reducing reliance on synthetic medications'; audience line 'Individuals with hypertension seeking natural alternatives'; testimonial heading 'Regaining Control Naturally'. · Cure framing in the FAQ: the question 'Can Ayurvedic treatment completely cure hypertension?' and the answer 'a complete cure depends on various factors and individual responses', which implies cure is possible for some. Also 'Ayurveda offers long-term solutions to manage and control blood pressure levels effectively.' · Outcome timeline in the FAQ: 'some clients experience improvements within a few weeks... several months of consistent treatment'. The physician must confirm or replace it, and reconcile it with the 14/21/28-day stay. · Efficacy claims in Benefits and Process: 'Balances blood pressure, reduces stress, and enhances cardiovascular health'; 'Improves circulation, supports healthy cholesterol'; 'Strengthens the immune system and addresses root causes of hypertension'; 'Customized herbal formulations will be prescribed to support cardiovascular health, regulate blood pressure, and reduce inflammation'; 'Custom herbal remedies support cardiovascular health and regulate blood pressure'. · Toxin and lasting-result claims: 'Ayurvedic procedures, herbal products, and massages ... eliminate toxins and restore health'; 'Ayurveda addresses hypertension at its roots, promoting lasting well-being and improved heart health'; 'Trust our expertise for holistic, effective hypertension management'; 'Ongoing support ensures long-term health'. · Patient testimonial as outcome evidence: 'by the time I left, my blood pressure had notably returned to normal' (Ms. Shalu, 5.0 stars). The owner must verify its provenance and consent. The physician must approve its use for a medical condition, with a 'results vary; not a substitute for your prescribed treatment' disclaimer. The 'Transformative Results' label also needs sign-off. · Therapy suitability for hypertensive patients. The generic inclusions promise 'Two Ayurvedic therapies per day, including herbal steam baths'; the routine names Udwarthana and 'a warm herbal bath'; the programme names Shirodhara, Thakradhara, Abhyangam and Nasyam. The physician must confirm which of these are used, adapted or excluded for hypertension (especially sudation/steam), and whether BP is checked before each therapy. · Yoga and pranayama in the routine ('Practice yoga asanas (postures) for 30-45 minutes to stimulate the body'; daily group yoga; pranayama). The physician or yoga lead must confirm these are adapted for hypertensive patients (e.g. inversions, breath retention) before this generic schedule is shown on a BP page. · Doctrinal statement 'addresses hypertension as a dosha imbalance, primarily in Pitta and Vata doshas', and Nasyam claimed to 'improve circulation'. The physician must confirm accuracy. · Scope implied by 'Cardiovascular Health' in the title and 'heart health' in the hero and meta. The physician must define which cardiovascular conditions the programme accepts and which it excludes (e.g. coronary disease, heart failure, arrhythmia, recent stroke), or narrow the naming to blood pressure. · Programme length '14/21/28 days' and the tariff's 'Minimum Duration is 14 Nights' for hypertension. The site-wide FAQ (signed off by Dr. Deepu John) says chronic-condition programmes run 'from 7 to 14 nights depending on the condition'. The physician must confirm the hypertension minimum and the criteria for 21 or 28 nights. · Whether this programme includes any Panchakarma: Nasyam is listed as a treatment, while 'specialty therapies (e.g., Panchakarma)' are billed as add-ons. The physician must state what is clinically included. · Related-programme links. If the page is reworked to link hypertension to stress, diabetes or weight-management programmes (instead of spine care), the physician should confirm those pairings are clinically appropriate.

### /treatment-for-nervous-disability/ — weak

Neurological and nervous-system conditions (Majjā Poshanam Chikitsa, "nervous disability"). The page frames these as Vata disorders. It covers general neuro function, stress-related nervous conditions and cognitive impairment, but it never names a single disease. The content pulls two ways. The About section, the "Who is this for" list and the testimonial ("neurological disease") describe clinical neurology. The hero, the meta description and the Benefits list sell stress relief and cognitive wellness. For the sitemap, the content backs its current placement in site-tree.json under Treatments & programmes > Nerves, paralysis & recovery, next to /treatment-for-paralysis/ and /treatment-for-facial-paralysis/. It also needs a cross-link from the stress group (/treatment-to-remove-stress-and-strain/, /yoga-based-stress-energy-reset-program/), because half the copy is about stress and cognition. Note on the snapshot: it is the mobile view (data-screen-label "Nervous Disability — Mobile"). The desktop view carries the same 3 FAQs. The page emits no FAQPage JSON-LD: schema.ts adds FAQPage only on /faqs-downloads/. Locked facts are correct: tel +91 9555 156 156, wa.me/919289838797, info@kairali.com, and no 38777 or 38794 anywhere.

FAQ questions on the page: 3.

**Proofreading (verified):**
- [locked-word] “Here are the questions guests most often ask before starting the neurological wellness & function programme.” — Banned word "guests" in the FAQ intro, on a clinical programme page. **Fix:** Here are the questions patients most often ask before starting the Neurological Wellness & Function Therapy programme.
- [overclaim-medical] “Yes, Ayurvedic treatment focuses on restoring balance to the body and mind, which can help alleviate nervous disabilities and promote overall well-being.” — The answer opens with an unqualified "Yes" on efficacy for a whole category of conditions, some of them progressive (Parkinson's, MS, dementia). It also does not answer the question it asks. **Fix:** Needs a physician-drafted answer: suitability depends on the specific diagnosis and its stage, the physician assesses at the free online consultation, and says plainly when a programme is not appropriate (the /online-consultation/ page already says this). Dr. Deepu John to sign off.
- [overclaim-medical] “Yes, Ayurvedic therapies and medications used in our retreat are made from natural herbs and minerals, minimizing the risk of adverse effects.” — An unqualified safety "Yes" that rests on "natural = safe". It contradicts FAQ 3, which admits that compatibility with other medicines has to be checked. "Retreat" breaks the hospital register. **Fix:** Physician-drafted answer on how medicines are selected, where they come from (/online-consultation/ names the group's WHO-GMP pharmacy), how contraindications are screened and how the patient is monitored. The answer never says the one thing a neuro patient must hear: do not stop or change prescribed medicines (anti-epileptics, levodopa, MS therapy, blood thinners after a stroke). **Fix:** Bring a complete medication list and recent neurologist letters.
