**Must-answer questions missing (12 of 25 gaps):**
- I take methotrexate / a biologic (e.g. adalimumab) / prednisolone / NSAIDs / a blood thinner. Must I stop them, and will your herbal medicines interact with them? — _Most 40–70-year-old RA patients from Europe, the USA or the Gulf are on DMARDs or biologics, and their rheumatologist will warn them about interactions. The page says it "reduces medication reliance" and appeals to people who "prefer natural… over conventional", which alarms exactly these patients. FAQ 4 only says "inform your doctor"._ Source: needs physician/owner (Dr. Deepu John: a written policy on continuing prescribed medicines and how interactions are screened)
- Is this programme for osteoarthritis, rheumatoid arthritis, or both, and how does the treatment differ? What about gout, psoriatic arthritis or ankylosing spondylitis? — _The page mixes OA and RA in one paragraph, names spondylitis only in the slug and two lines, and has a sibling /treatment-for-spondylitis/ page. A patient can't tell whether they are on the right page._ Source: this page, partly (About: RA gets a body-cleansing plan with Elakizhi and Pizhichil); the rest needs physician/owner. Sibling pages: /treatment-for-spondylitis/, /treatment-for-cervical-spondylosis/
- I have a knee or hip replacement, metal implants, severe deformity, or I am on a waiting list for surgery. Can I still be admitted, and which therapies are unsuitable? — _Joint replacement is very common in this age group, and heat, oil and poultice therapies near implants are an obvious worry._ Source: needs physician/owner (admission criteria and contraindications)
- I am 68 with high blood pressure / diabetes / heart disease. Are two heat and oil therapies a day (Pizhichil, Elakizhi, steam) safe for me? Who decides I am fit for admission? — _Comorbidities are the norm at 40–70, and heat therapies are the most visible part of the programme._ Source: FAQs & Downloads q-35 (senior considerations, medical monitoring) and q-47 (no stated maximum age); fitness and contraindication criteria need physician/owner
- What can I realistically expect after 14 nights, and how long does it last? Could my joints flare during treatment? — _This is the core buying question for a long-haul trip. FAQ 3 only says "a few weeks", which can be longer than the stay, and the testimonial speaks of a "new lease on life"._ Source: needs physician/owner (no outcome figures may be invented); FAQs & Downloads q-15 covers mild detox symptoms in general
- What will the total cost be for arthritis? Will I need Panchakarma or lab tests at extra cost, and roughly how much do the medicines cost? — _The page says "All-Inclusive… No hidden fees" but also says medicines, tests and Panchakarma cost extra, and FAQs & Downloads q-18 says medicines are included. RA patients get a "body cleansing plan" with no price._ Source: this page, partly (tariffs, add-ons); the medicine, lab and Panchakarma add-on costs and policy need physician/owner
- Which tests do you do or need (RA factor, anti-CCP, ESR/CRP, uric acid, X-ray/MRI)? Should I send them before booking? — _Western patients expect lab and imaging-based diagnosis. The page describes only pulse and visual examination, and "recent reports" is unspecified._ Source: needs physician/owner (report checklist); /online-consultation/ and FAQs & Downloads q-45 (free first online consultation) for sending reports
- Who will my physician be, what are their qualifications, and do they speak English, German, French, Russian or Arabic? — _The page names no physician, says the "Doctor might change", and mentions "multilingual staff" without listing any language._ Source: FAQs & Downloads q-06 and q-11 (BAMS-qualified physicians; the Chief Physician oversees all programmes); the named physician and the languages spoken need physician/owner
- What does a treatment day look like for someone with painful knees or hands: which therapies, how long, and how much yoga and walking? — _The routine shown is generic to every programme (30–45 min asana at 06:30, a nature walk, 17:00 yoga, one therapy slot) and conflicts with "2 therapies per day"._ Source: needs physician/owner (an arthritis-specific day plan); this page, partly (routine, Daily Activities)
- Can I get around the campus with a stick, walker or wheelchair? Are the villas step-free with grab rails? How far is it from my villa to the treatment rooms? Is there help getting on and off therapy tables? — _Mobility is the condition itself. Oil therapies make floors and tables slippery, and falls are a major fear at 60+._ Source: FAQs & Downloads q-35 (ramps, mobility assistance); villa-level accessibility needs physician/owner
- If my condition flares, I fall ill, or it doesn't help and I need to leave early, do I get anything back? — _The page requires 100% advance payment and gives no refund for cancellations under 15 days or a date change during the stay, which is a heavy risk for a long-haul patient with a fluctuating disease._ Source: this page (T&C) and /cancellation-and-refund-policy/; the medical early-discharge policy needs physician/owner
- Is this a real hospital with a physician on site at night and emergency backup, or a wellness hotel? — _About 30 spa, luxury and resort award cards, "serene retreat" and "Luxury Stay" dominate the page, while the only clinical proof is one NABH line. A family member worried about a 70-year-old parent will ask this first._ Source: FAQs & Downloads q-01 (NABH-accredited hospital) and q-37 (arrangements with nearby hospitals); night physician cover and the nearest multi-specialty hospital with distance need physician/owner

**For Dr. Deepu John:** FAQ 1: "Ayurvedic treatment has been proven effective in managing arthritis by reducing pain, and inflammation, and improving joint mobility." This is an absolute efficacy claim with no evidence. · FAQ 2: "Ayurvedic therapies and medications are generally safe… We prioritize the use of natural herbs to minimize side effects." This safety statement conflicts with Process step 05, which says the medicines are made from "natural herbs and minerals". The physician must confirm how mineral-containing formulations are selected and monitored. · FAQ 3: "Typically, noticeable improvements can be observed within a few weeks". An outcome timeline for OA and RA needs physician wording. · FAQ 4: "Ayurvedic treatment can be integrated with conventional medicine." This needs an explicit physician policy for DMARDs, biologics, corticosteroids, NSAIDs and anticoagulants, including a line never to stop prescribed medicines without the prescribing doctor. · Benefits: "reduces medication reliance" is effectively stop-medication advice for RA and OA patients. · Who For: "Individuals who prefer natural therapies and medications over conventional treatments" may lead RA patients to abandon DMARDs. · Who For: "treat the underlying causes of the issue rather than just the symptoms" claims root-cause resolution for degenerative and autoimmune disease. · About: "If you’re experiencing joint pain, fever, fatigue, and loss of appetite, it could be Arthritis" encourages self-diagnosis. Fever with joint pain needs urgent medical assessment (possible infection). · About: "immune system strengthening" is presented as an aim for a programme that includes rheumatoid arthritis, which is autoimmune. · About: "For Rheumatoid Arthritis, a body cleansing plan is employed along with therapies like Elakizhi, Pizhichil" is a stated treatment protocol. The physician must confirm it and say whether the cleansing is chargeable Panchakarma. · Subtitle "Sandhivātam Chikitsa… { Osteo & Rheumatoid }": in classical usage Sandhivata generally corresponds to osteoarthritis, while RA is usually described as Amavata. The physician must confirm the Sanskrit label. · Hero/meta: "Reduce joint pain, inflammation & improve mobility" and Benefits "Reduced pain, inflammation, and stiffness while enhancing joint mobility… Improved overall quality of life" state outcomes as certain. · Our Approach: "Trust in our expertise for lasting relief and improved vitality" promises a lasting outcome. · Process step 02: diagnosis by "pulse examination (Nadi Pariksha) and visual examination (Darshana Pariksha)… identify the root cause". The physician should state what lab or imaging review applies for arthritis. · Process step 05: "Ayurvedic medications made from natural herbs and minerals". Mineral content, safety testing and home-country import legality for international patients need physician confirmation. · Routine: yoga asanas for 30–45 minutes at 06:30, a nature walk, 17:00 yoga, an "herbal bath" and "Detoxifying Drink… to kickstart the metabolism". This generic day plan needs physician confirmation for arthritis patients, including heat and steam therapies in actively inflamed RA joints. · Testimonial: "left with a new lease on life… effectively reduced my inflammation… managing my osteoarthritis". A patient-outcome testimonial on a hospital page needs verification against the source video, patient consent, and an "individual results vary" line. · Sunday session: "prevent diseases naturally" is a disease-prevention claim. · FAQ intro and page-wide: any rewritten arthritis FAQ answers (medication, timelines, contraindications, eligibility for implants, comorbidities, flares) must be drafted from Dr. Deepu John's input and signed off before staging goes to production.

### /treatment-for-chronic-back-pain/ — weak

Chronic back pain (Kaṭi Shūla Chikitsa). The page's "Who is this for" list covers degenerative disc disease, herniated or bulging discs, sciatica, muscle spasm, postural, arthritis-related, stress-related and nerve-impingement back pain. It is an inpatient programme sold as 14/21/28-day packages with a 14-night minimum. Note: disc herniation and sciatica overlap with the sibling pages /treatment-for-slip-disc/ and /treatment-for-spondylitis/.

FAQ questions on the page: 4.

**Proofreading (verified):**
- [locked-word] “Here are the questions guests most often ask before starting the chronic back pain relief programme.” — The FAQ intro uses the banned word "guests". The claim that these are the questions people "most often ask" is also unsupported, since three of the four are generic. **Fix:** "Questions patients ask before admission to the chronic back pain programme."
- [locked-word] “World Luxury Award Winner” — This is in the trust strip under the hero and again above the footer CTA. It uses the banned word "Luxury", and it paraphrases award titles (World Luxury Spa Awards / World Luxury Hotel Awards) into a name no award actually has, placed next to the NABH claim. **Fix:** Remove it, or replace it with a clinical trust signal the page already states ("NABH-accredited for Panchakarma"). How awards are shown is the owner's call.
- [locked-word] “Luxury, vastu-compliant villas with modern amenities” — Banned word "Luxury" in the Inclusions list. **Fix:** "Vastu-compliant villas with modern amenities"
- [locked-word] “Premium luxury experience” — Banned word in the Royal Villa tagline on the tariff card. **Fix:** Remove the tagline, or describe the villa using facts from the /royal-villa/ page (owner to supply).
- [locked-word] “Luxury Stay: Unwind in private, eco-friendly villas designed for tranquility” — Banned word "Luxury" in the Inclusions heading. The item is also written in holiday-stay register. **Fix:** "Accommodation: a private, eco-friendly villa for the length of your admission."
- [locked-word] “Inclusions — Your Holistic Wellness Journey Includes” — Banned word "Journey" in a section heading. **Fix:** "Inclusions — What Your Programme Includes"
- [locked-word] “with continued doctor support for a safe and effective journey.” — Banned word "journey". "Safe and effective" is also an unqualified outcome promise. **Fix:** "with continued physician supervision throughout your treatment."
- [locked-word] “Daily wellness check-ins with your Ayurvedic doctor to tailor your journey.” — Banned word "journey". **Fix:** "Daily physician review to adjust your treatment."
- [locked-word] “Learn about the journey from seed to medicine.” — Banned word "journey" in the Monday farm-visit card. The lock bans the word in any sense. **Fix:** "See how medicinal herbs are grown and processed into medicine."
- [locked-word] “on a reclining guest’s lower back during Kati Vasti” — Banned word "guest" in the hero image's aria-label. Screen readers read it out and search engines index it. **Fix:** "...on a reclining patient’s lower back during Kati Vasti"
- [locked-word] “Acknowledged for its outstanding service and guest satisfaction” — Banned word "guest" in the descriptions on the Certificate of Excellence 2018 and 2019 cards. These are descriptions, not award names. **Fix:** "...outstanding service and patient satisfaction". Award names that contain Spa/Luxury/Resort (World Luxury Spa Awards, 'Wellness Resort of the Year', 'India’s Leading Spa Resort') are left for a human to judge, per DECISIONS.md.
- [locked-word] “Celebrated as a leading wellness and spa brand” — Banned word "spa" in the description on the South Asian Travel Awards 2018 card. This is not the award's title. **Fix:** "Recognised at the South Asian Travel Awards 2018."
- [locked-word] “socializing with fellow retreat participants” — Wrong register, but not a banned word. "Retreat participants", "settle into your serene retreat", the footer's "A serene retreat in India" and "Check-in / Check-out" all frame a hospital stay as a holiday. The required register is patient, admission, discharge. **Fix:** "fellow patients"; "as you settle in after admission"; "Admission: 12:00 noon / Discharge: 10:00 AM". Owner to rule on the word "retreat" in the footer.
- [contradiction] “Internal Medicines and test reports needed for your treatment can only be ascertained as your treatment progresses and will cost extra.” — Contradiction. The About section says "Treatment includes Ayurvedic medicines". This T&C says medicines cost extra. The FAQs & Downloads page lists "Herbal medicines and formulations during your stay" as included in the package. **Fix:** Owner to decide one rule on whether in-stay medicines are included and apply it on every page. Until then, do not describe medicines as included.
- [contradiction] “All-Inclusive Pricing: No hidden fees! Includes accommodation, meals, therapies, and applicable taxes.” — The same page says medicines, tests, extra food, laundry, telephone and internet cost extra, and lists Panchakarma as an add-on. So "All-inclusive / No hidden fees" is not true as written. (FAQs & Downloads also says Wi-Fi in common areas is included.) **Fix:** "The price includes accommodation, three meals, the prescribed daily therapies and taxes. Medicines, tests, additional therapies, laundry and [owner to list] are charged separately."
- [contradiction] “Transparent Add-Ons: Internal herbal medicines, lab tests, or specialty therapies (e.g., Panchakarma) available as needed.” — Panchakarma is listed as an extra-cost add-on. Yet the About section lists Vasti (a Panchakarma procedure) as part of the treatment, and the hospital's NABH accreditation is for Panchakarma. A patient cannot tell whether Kati Vasti or Vasti is inside the price. **Fix:** Physician/owner to state which back-pain therapies fall within the 2 a day and which are billed extra.
- [contradiction] “Support: Airport transfers & multilingual staff” — Under "What We Offer" this reads as included. The Inclusions list does not mention transfers, and FAQs & Downloads says airport/railway transfers are extra (Coimbatore ₹6,775 one way). The languages spoken are not named. **Fix:** "Airport transfers can be arranged at extra cost (see How to Reach)." Owner to name the languages the staff and physicians speak.
- [contradiction] “The above rates are valid from April 1, 2025, to October 31, 2026.” — FAQs & Downloads quotes the same extra-bed/meal amounts and transfer rates as "valid 1 Oct 2026 – 30 Sep 2027", so the two windows overlap. This tariff also runs out about 5 weeks after this audit (23 Sep 2026), so patients planning a winter stay have no valid price. **Fix:** Owner to confirm the current rate card and its validity dates and publish one window on every page.
- [overclaim-medical] “Treatment includes Ayurvedic medicines, herbal massages (Abhyangam, Vasti, Elakizhi, Kativasti), meditation, and Yoga to rejuvenate tissues, fortify bones, and relieve pain.” — Vasti (a medicated enema, a Panchakarma procedure) is not a massage. "Fortify bones" is an unsupported structural claim. The therapy list also differs across sections: Approach and Process give Abhyangam, Pizhichil, Kati Basti, Nasya; the routine gives Udwarthana. It is spelled Kativasti, Kati Basti and Kati Vasti, and both Abhyangam and Abhyanga appear. **Fix:** Physician to provide one canonical back-pain protocol list with one spelling per therapy. Drop "fortify bones" unless the physician substantiates it.
- [overclaim-medical] “Yes, Ayurvedic treatment can be highly effective for chronic back pain. It addresses the root causes of pain, reduces inflammation, strengthens the spine, and promotes healing.” — FAQ overclaim: an unqualified "Yes... highly effective", and "strengthens the spine" is presented as fact. There is no line saying red-flag symptoms need orthopaedic or neurological evaluation. **Fix:** Physician-approved wording that describes what the programme aims to do, not guaranteed effects. Update the verbatim FAQPage JSON-LD in public/ahv/views/ahv-page-chronic-back-pain-{desktop,mobile}.json at the same time; it is not in the prerender snapshot.
- [overclaim-medical] “Yes, the therapies and medications used in Herbal Back Pain Management are generally safe when administered by trained professionals.” — The answer opens with an unconditional "Yes". "Herbal Back Pain Management" is a stray keyword/product name that appears nowhere else on the page. It says nothing about screening, contraindications or interactions with existing drugs. **Fix:** Rename to "this programme". Physician to write an answer covering the admission assessment, the review of existing medication, and the conditions in which therapies are modified or avoided. Update the JSON-LD too.
- [overclaim-medical] “They are derived from natural ingredients and prepared with careful attention to quality and purity.” — Implies "natural" means safe. Process step 04 adds "derived from potent herbs and minerals, are chosen for their safety and effectiveness". EU and US patients will ask about heavy metals, and none of the quality claims is backed by evidence. **Fix:** Physician/owner to state how the medicines are made and tested (GMP, heavy-metal testing) only if true. Otherwise remove the quality claims.
- [overclaim-medical] “While some individuals may experience relief within a few sessions, others may require a longer treatment period.” — Gives a relief timeline ("within a few sessions") and ends with "speed up the healing process". Both are outcome claims. The answer also never relates results to the 14/21/28-night packages. **Fix:** Physician-approved answer. Avoid "healing process". Mention a possible temporary flare only if the physician confirms it happens. Update the JSON-LD.
- [wrong-fact] “The World Health Organization reports traditional medicine in use across more than 170 of its 194 member states.” — WHO's published figure (Global Report on Traditional and Complementary Medicine, 2019) is 170 Member States, not "more than 170". The statistic has no citation and nothing to do with back pain. **Fix:** "...170 of its 194 Member States (WHO, 2019)" with a source link, or drop it. Verify against the WHO source before publishing.
- [overclaim-medical] “Herbal remedies reduce inflammation, nourish tissues, and strengthen the spine.” — Mechanism and outcome claims stated as fact. **Fix:** Physician sign-off, or soften to intent ("are prescribed to...").
- [overclaim-medical] “where Ayurveda empowers a pain-free life with enhanced well-being.” — Promises a "pain-free" outcome. The same paragraph also claims it "prevents future issues". **Fix:** Remove "pain-free" and "prevents". For example: "aims to reduce pain and help you manage it long term", subject to physician approval.
- [overclaim-medical] “Holistic approach targeting root causes for lasting relief and well-being.” — Promises lasting relief. It sits alongside "Effective, holistic relief from chronic back pain", "sustainable results" and "Ongoing support ensures long-term health". **Fix:** Physician-approved benefit wording with no guaranteed durability.
- [overclaim-medical] “I feel rejuvenated and pain-free for the first time in years.” — The testimonial makes outcome claims ("pain-free", "feel like I have my life back") under the heading "Transformative Results", with 5.0 stars. Where the testimonial came from and whether the patient consented are not shown. **Fix:** Owner to verify the source and consent. Rename the heading "Patient experiences". Physician to sign off on outcome wording.
- [contradiction] “Ms. Amritha & Ms. Eva” — Two names are credited to a first-person singular testimonial ("The chronic back pain I lived with..."). **Fix:** Owner to confirm who wrote it and credit one person.
- [wrong-fact] “NABH Certified Hospital” — NABH accredits rather than certifies. The award card on the same page says "NABH-accredited for Panchakarma", so the trust strip misnames the accreditation and its scope. **Fix:** "NABH-accredited Ayurvedic hospital" (the FAQs & Downloads wording). Owner to confirm the scope wording.
- [unclear] “Kairali – The Ayurvedic Healing Village offers 14/21/28-day packages targeting dosha equilibrium.” — Packages are given in days here but the tariff says "Minimum Duration is 14 Nights". Nothing tells a patient how to choose between 14, 21 and 28. FAQs & Downloads says musculoskeletal programmes are 14 nights. **Fix:** Use nights throughout. Physician/owner to state what decides 14, 21 or 28 for back pain, and confirm that 21 and 28 exist for this programme.
- [unclear] “(Note: Preventive Health & Regenerative Program includes one therapy per day.)” — A note about a different programme, copied into the back-pain Inclusions. It is irrelevant here and confusing. **Fix:** Remove it from this page.
- [grammar] “118+ years of Ayurvedic expertise at Kairali – The Ayurvedic Healing Village, it specializes in chronic back pain.” — Dangling subject ("With... at Kairali..., it specializes"). It also attaches the 1908 lineage to the campus's back-pain expertise. **Fix:** "Drawing on the Kairali family's Ayurvedic practice since 1908, Kairali – The Ayurvedic Healing Village treats chronic back pain..."
- [grammar] “Our Holistic back pain relief therapy starts with an assessment for a tailored plan.” — Stray capital H. "Therapy" should be "programme" (register). **Fix:** "Our back pain programme begins with a physician assessment and a tailored plan."
- [grammar] “Why one should opt for this therapy?” — Ungrammatical question. **Fix:** "Why choose this programme?"
- [typo] “Who is This Program For?” — Inconsistent capitalisation and US spelling, while the page elsewhere uses "programme". Also mixed: "Program details", "Personalized" vs "Individualised", "Discover More Customizable Programs". **Fix:** "Who Is This Programme For?" Standardise on UK spelling across the page.
- [typo] “MORE PROGRAMs” — Casing typo in the related-programmes eyebrow. **Fix:** "MORE PROGRAMMES"
- [grammar] “Doctor might change as subject to availability.” — Ungrammatical. **Fix:** "Your consulting doctor may change, subject to availability."
- [grammar] “Up to less than 15 days before arrival date or Change in Date during Stay – No Refund.” — "Up to less than" is ungrammatical, and the bands are ambiguous ("Up to 30 days" / "Up to 15 days"). FAQs & Downloads puts them as 30+ full, 15–30 50%, under 15 none, but "15–30" overlaps at 30. **Fix:** "30 or more days before admission: full refund. 15–29 days: 50% refund. Fewer than 15 days, or a date change during your stay: no refund." Owner to confirm where the boundaries fall.
- [typo] “The above rates are inclusive of applicable taxes*.” — The asterisk has no footnote. **Fix:** Remove the asterisk or add the footnote (owner).
- [typo] “Worlds Luxury Spa Awards Winner 2024” — "Worlds" should be "World" (also in "Worlds Luxury Hotel Awards 2024"); other cards say "World Luxury Spa Awards". The proper noun contains banned words, which is left for a human to judge. **Fix:** "World Luxury Spa Awards 2024" / "World Luxury Hotel Awards 2024", if the owner keeps them.
- [typo] “K.V. Ramesh, Managing Director of Kairali Ayurvedic Group, was honored with the CONSIDICI National Award in 2019.” — The card title says "COSIDICI National Award 2019" but the body says "CONSIDICI". **Fix:** Use "COSIDICI" in both places.
- [typo] “Best Asian Healthcare Brands 2017 by The Economic Times.” — The card title says "Brand" and the body says "Brands". Similarly the ATTO card has "Organisations" in the title and "Organisation" in the body. **Fix:** Make the title and body match (owner to confirm the exact award name).
- [unclear] “History” — The in-page nav tab "History" jumps to the "Our Approach" section (id=history). There is no history content. **Fix:** Rename the tab "Approach".
- [overclaim-medical] “Relieve chronic back pain naturally with Ayurvedic therapies, yoga & lifestyle care at Kairali – The Ayurvedic Healing Village. Book your healing today.” — "Book your healing today" in the hero lede and meta description sells an outcome. The og:title in route-manifest.json is also broken: "Chronic Back Pain Relief TreatmentKati Shula Chikitsa ( Kairali’s Treatment for Chronic Back Pain)". **Fix:** "...Book a free doctor consultation today." Fix the og:title to "Chronic Back Pain Relief Treatment – Kaṭi Shūla Chikitsa".

**Must-answer questions missing (10 of 23 gaps):**
- I take painkillers (NSAIDs, pregabalin/gabapentin, opioids), muscle relaxants, blood thinners or blood-pressure or diabetes medicines. Do I stop them, and will your herbal medicines interact with them? — _This is the first worry for a 40–70 patient on long-term pain and chronic-disease medicines. The page only says "Bring: ... medications" and has no FAQ on it. Without an answer, their GP will tell them not to go._ Source: needs physician/owner (Dr. Deepu John): the policy on reviewing and continuing existing medication, and on checking interactions.
- My problem is a herniated disc, sciatica or spinal stenosis. Is this programme right for me, or the Spinal Disc & Nerve Restoration or Spine Care & Spondylitis programme? — _This page lists herniated discs and sciatica, but /treatment-for-slip-disc/ covers the same patients, and the related-programmes block does not link to it. The patient cannot tell which to book._ Source: Other site pages: /treatment-for-slip-disc/ and /treatment-for-spondylitis/ for scope. The routing rule itself needs physician/owner.
- Is it safe if I have had spine surgery (fusion, screws, disc replacement) or have osteoporosis? Can Kati Vasti, Elakizhi (hot poultice) or Pizhichil be done over implants or on fragile bones? — _Many chronic back pain patients over 50 have hardware or low bone density. The page promises heat and oil therapies but says nothing about when they are modified._ Source: needs physician/owner
- Who should NOT come, and when must I see an orthopaedic surgeon or neurologist first (bladder or bowel changes, worsening leg weakness, fever, cancer history, recent fall)? Do you need my MRI before admission? — _The page lists "leg discomfort, and numbness" as ordinary symptoms but gives no red-flag or exclusion guidance. A safety-minded patient reads that as either unsafe or not serious._ Source: needs physician/owner
- I am 65 with high blood pressure, diabetes or heart disease. Can I safely take daily heat and oil therapies and long sessions lying face down? — _The page does not mention age or comorbidities._ Source: The FAQs & Downloads page has a generic seniors answer (modified treatments, medical monitoring, emergency services on call, no stated maximum age). The condition-specific answer needs physician/owner.
- Do I need 14, 21 or 28 nights for my back, and when should I expect to feel a difference? Can the pain get worse before it improves? — _The page offers three lengths with no way to choose. The only timing FAQ is vague ("within a few sessions"). Patients booking long-haul leave need to plan._ Source: The FAQs & Downloads page says musculoskeletal programmes are 14 nights and the physician confirms at consultation. Timelines and flare-ups need physician/owner, and no outcome may be invented.
- What will the whole stay cost me? Are herbal medicines, lab tests, Kati Vasti/Vasti and the airport transfer included, or extra? — _The page contradicts itself ("All-Inclusive... No hidden fees" versus medicines and tests extra) and contradicts FAQs & Downloads (medicines included). An unknown bill stops a 100%-advance booking._ Source: needs physician/owner (which items are included, and a typical range for medicines and tests)
- Who will be my doctor? What are their qualifications and experience with spine conditions, and do they speak English (or German, French, Russian, Arabic)? — _The page says "experienced Ayurvedic doctor" and "multilingual staff" but gives no names, qualifications or languages. A European or Russian patient needs to know they will be understood._ Source: The FAQs & Downloads page explains BAMS/MD Ayurveda training generically. Named physicians, their spine experience and the languages spoken need owner.
- What does a back-pain treatment day actually involve: which two therapies, how long, how physical, and how much yoga with a painful back? — _The day plan shown is a generic detox template: one therapy slot, Udwarthana "to promote detoxification", 30–45 minutes of yoga at 06:30 plus a nature walk. It does not match the back-pain protocol, and it worries someone who cannot bend._ Source: needs physician/owner (a condition-specific sample day)
- Is this a hospital or a spa holiday? The awards on the page say "Spa", "Luxury" and "Resort". — _A medical traveller, and their insurer or GP, needs to see a hospital. More than 25 award cards with spa/luxury/resort names sit above the treatment process and undercut the NABH claim._ Source: This page (the NABH card: accredited for Panchakarma, certificate AH-2024-0250) and FAQs & Downloads ("NABH-accredited Ayurvedic hospital... BAMS-qualified physicians"). How awards are presented needs owner.

**For Dr. Deepu John:** FAQ efficacy claim: "Yes, Ayurvedic treatment can be highly effective for chronic back pain... reduces inflammation, strengthens the spine, and promotes healing." Also appears in the FAQPage JSON-LD. · FAQ safety claim: an unconditional "Yes... generally safe" and "derived from natural ingredients". It says nothing about contraindications, screening, or interactions with NSAIDs, gabapentinoids, opioids or anticoagulants. · FAQ timeline claim: "some individuals may experience relief within a few sessions" and "speed up the healing process". · Process step 04: "derived from potent herbs and minerals, are chosen for their safety and effectiveness". The herbo-mineral content, quality and heavy-metal testing claims need physician confirmation. · The therapy protocol for back pain differs across sections: Abhyangam, Vasti, Elakizhi, Kativasti in About; Abhyangam, Pizhichil, Kati Basti, Nasya in Approach and Process; Udwarthana "to promote detoxification" in the routine. The physician must confirm the actual protocol, including whether Nasya and Udwarthana are indicated, and that Vasti (an enema) is not a "massage". · "to rejuvenate tissues, fortify bones, and relieve pain" and "Herbal remedies reduce inflammation, nourish tissues, and strengthen the spine" are structural and mechanistic claims about bone and spine. · "Ayurveda offers a holistic, natural, non-invasive approach that alleviates symptoms and prevents future issues" and "empowers a pain-free life" claim prevention and a pain-free outcome. · Benefits and Why-choose lists: "Effective, holistic relief", "lasting relief", "sustainable results", "Ongoing support ensures long-term health". · Suitability list: that degenerative disc disease, herniated/bulging discs, sciatica and nerve impingement are appropriate for this programme. It needs exclusion and red-flag criteria and a rule on referring to the Spinal Disc & Nerve Restoration programme. · Symptoms paragraph lists "leg discomfort, and numbness" with no red-flag advice (cauda equina signs, progressive weakness, fever, malignancy, trauma) and no statement that the programme does not replace orthopaedic or neurological evaluation. · Testimonials: "pain-free for the first time in years", "feel like I have my life back", heading "Transformative Results", 5.0 stars. Patient-outcome claims need physician sign-off, and the owner must verify the source and consent (two names on one first-person quote). · Daily routine: "Practice yoga asanas (postures) for 30-45 minutes" at 06:30, another yoga session at 17:00, and a daily nature walk. Physician to confirm this is safe or adapted for patients with acute or severe back pain. · Programme length: whether 14/21/28 nights applies to back pain and what decides the length. FAQs & Downloads says musculoskeletal programmes are 14 nights. · "with continued doctor support for a safe and effective journey" (an unqualified safe/effective promise) and the Pre-Program "design preparatory steps". The physician must say what preparation is asked of patients, e.g. any change to current medication before arrival. · "Detoxifying Drink... to kickstart the metabolism" is a physiological claim in the routine. · Every medical-content change here is EN-only (DE/FR show "coming soon"), so each fix ships flagged as EN-only pending translation. Dr. Deepu John must sign off before staging goes to production.

### /post-pregnancy-health-programme/ — weak

Postpartum (post-pregnancy) recovery for new mothers, branded "Postpartum Rejuvenation Therapy / Sutika Paricharya". Stated aims: rebuild strength and energy, support lactation, heal and 'restore' the pelvic structure, relieve neck and back ache, and reduce stress. Per the FAQ, a mother is admitted only from 45 days after a normal delivery or 120 days after a caesarean. The About section gives 14/21/28 days, extendable to 40; the tariff sets a 14-night minimum.

FAQ questions on the page: 4.

**Proofreading (verified):**
- [locked-word] “Motherhood is an incredible journey, but it’s important to understand that your well-being continues to be a priority after giving birth.” — Uses the banned word "journey" in the opening line of the About section. **Fix:** "Motherhood brings profound change, and your own health remains a priority after giving birth."
- [locked-word] “provide ongoing support and guidance throughout the post-pregnancy journey.” — Banned word "journey" (Process step 06). **Fix:** "...throughout the post-pregnancy period."
- [locked-word] “with continued doctor support for a safe and effective journey.” — Banned word "journey" (Doctor Guidance, During Treatment). **Fix:** "...with continued physician support throughout your treatment."
- [locked-word] “Inclusions — Your Holistic Wellness Journey Includes” — Banned word "Journey" in the tariff inclusions heading. **Fix:** "Inclusions — what your programme includes"
- [locked-word] “Daily wellness check-ins with your Ayurvedic doctor to tailor your journey.” — Banned word "journey". Also uses the "wellness check-in" register where a daily physician consultation is meant. **Fix:** "Daily consultation with your Ayurvedic physician to adjust your treatment."
- [locked-word] “Learn about the journey from seed to medicine.” — Banned word "journey" (Monday farm visit card). **Fix:** "Learn how a medicinal plant goes from seed to medicine."
- [locked-word] “World Luxury Award Winner” — Banned word "Luxury" in the page's own trust strip. It appears twice, below the About section and in the closing band. This is Kairali's own claim, not an award's proper name. **Fix:** Replace it with "Internationally awarded", or name one verified award. The owner decides whether award proper names containing Luxury or Spa may appear at all on KTAHV pages.
- [locked-word] “Luxury, vastu-compliant villas with modern amenities” — Banned word "Luxury" (Offered Package, Accommodation & Nutrition). **Fix:** "Private, vastu-compliant villas with modern amenities"
- [locked-word] “Luxury Stay: Unwind in private, eco-friendly villas designed for tranquility” — Banned word "Luxury", plus holiday framing ("Unwind") on a clinical admission page. **Fix:** "Accommodation: private, eco-friendly villas on the hospital campus."
- [locked-word] “Premium luxury experience” — Banned word "luxury" in the Royal Villa tariff label. **Fix:** Describe the villa's actual features, such as size or layout, and get the owner to supply the facts.
- [locked-word] “Acknowledged for its outstanding service and guest satisfaction” — Banned word "guest". It appears twice, in the Certificate of Excellence 2018 and 2019 descriptions. **Fix:** "...outstanding service and patient satisfaction"
- [locked-word] “honored with the prestigious World Travel Award 2016 as India’s Leading Spa Resort.” — Banned words "Spa" and "Resort". The shared awards block on this page also carries "crowned ‘Wellness Resort of the Year’", "leading wellness and spa brand", "Best Luxury Ayurveda Spa" and every "World Luxury Spa Awards" title. The language-lock audit counts 8 luxury and 8 journey hits on this route. **Fix:** Escalate to the owner. Decide whether award proper names are exempt; if they are not, filter these awards off KTAHV clinical pages. Rewrite the descriptive text Kairali wrote itself either way.
- [locked-word] “socializing with fellow retreat participants.” — Uses the hospitality register ("retreat participants"); the required register is "patients" (daily routine, 19:30). **Fix:** "...or spending time with other patients."
- [unclear] “Postpartum wellness enthusiasts” — The "Who is This Program For?" list names a wellness-hobbyist audience for a physician-admitted programme (wellness register). The heading's capitalisation is also inconsistent: "is" lowercase, "This" capitalised. **Fix:** Remove it, or use "Mothers who want physician-supervised postnatal recovery". Heading: "Who is this programme for?"
- [grammar] “Sutika Paricharya ( Post Pregnancy Health Program )” — There are spaces inside the parentheses, "Post-Pregnancy" is missing its hyphen, and "Program" breaks the required "programme" register. The page mixes about 15 uses of "Program" with "Individualised Programmes". The og:title has the same text run together as "Postpartum Rejuvenation TherapySutika Paricharya ( Post Pregnancy Health Program )". **Fix:** "Sutika Paricharya (Post-Pregnancy Health Programme)". Use "programme" throughout, and add the missing space and fix the parentheses in og:title.
- [contradiction] “Our postpartum rejuvenation therapy in Ayurveda package includes Sootika Charya, a therapeutic post-pregnancy massage, and a balanced diet.” — The About section transliterates the name as "Sootika Charya" while the hero says "Sutika Paricharya". The sentence is also keyword-stuffed ("therapy in Ayurveda package"). **Fix:** "The programme includes Sutika Paricharya (classical postnatal care), a therapeutic post-pregnancy massage and a balanced diet."
- [contradiction] “The Natural Postpartum Recovery Therapy program duration may vary depending on individual needs and progress.” — The FAQ gives the programme a third name that appears nowhere else on the page. **Fix:** "The duration of the Postpartum Rejuvenation Therapy programme..."
- [contradiction] “It can range from two weeks to four weeks, as determined by the Ayurvedic doctor.” — Contradicts the About section: "lasting 14/21/28 days, extendable to 40 days". The FAQ and the JSON-LD leave out the 40-day option. **Fix:** Align it with About: "14, 21 or 28 days, extendable to 40 days, as decided by your physician." The physician should confirm the 40-day option.
- [contradiction] “These treatments can be availed only after 45 days following a normal delivery.” — The hero names the programme "Sutika Paricharya", and the History section builds on the "Sutika Kala" regimen, which is the care given in the weeks right after birth. Yet admission starts only once that 45-day window is over (120 days after a caesarean). A reader cannot tell whether this is the classical sutika regimen or a later rejuvenation programme. "Availed" is also Indian-English usage, and the next sentence spells "cesarean" in US style on a British-spelling page. **Fix:** The physician should define what the programme is relative to the sutika kala, for example "a post-sutika rejuvenation programme". Then use "can begin" and "caesarean".
- [contradiction] “Yes, the program emphasizes a balanced and nourishing diet.” — The answer opens with "Yes" and then says there are "generally no extreme dietary restrictions". It also leaves out restrictions the page states elsewhere: meals are vegetarian or vegan only, and coffee and alcohol are forbidden (T&C: "Abstinence from Smoking, Alcohol, & Coffee"). **Fix:** State plainly that meals are vegetarian (vegan on request) and physician-prescribed, and that coffee, alcohol and smoking are not permitted. The physician should add the lactation-specific guidance.
- [contradiction] “The program includes dietary recommendations and herbal supplements that support healthy lactation and breastfeeding.” — The FAQ says the herbal supplements are included, but the T&C says "Internal Medicines and test reports ... will cost extra" and the Inclusions list says internal herbal medicines are "available as needed". A patient cannot tell whether the lactation herbs are in the price. **Fix:** The owner should confirm whether postpartum or lactation formulations are included or billed. Then make the FAQ, the Inclusions list and the T&C say the same thing.
- [contradiction] “All-Inclusive Pricing: No hidden fees! Includes accommodation, meals, therapies, and applicable taxes.” — The same tariff block charges extra for internal medicines, lab tests, extra food, laundry, private yoga and specialty therapies, so "All-Inclusive" and "No hidden fees" are not accurate. The next sentence ("Rates are subject to change") also undercuts it. **Fix:** "The package rate includes accommodation, meals, prescribed therapies and taxes. Internal medicines, lab tests and the services listed below are charged separately."
- [contradiction] “Support: Airport transfers & multilingual staff” — This sits under "Inclusions", which implies the transfer is free. The site's FAQs & Downloads page says transfers cost extra (₹6,775 MPV, one way). The page never says which languages the staff speak. **Fix:** "Airport transfers can be arranged at extra cost (see How to Reach)." The owner should name the languages spoken, or drop "multilingual".
- [wrong-fact] “The above rates are valid from April 1, 2025, to October 31, 2026.” — The tariff runs out in about 5 weeks (today is 23 Sep 2026). A mother planning to arrive 45–120 days after delivery will almost certainly come after 31 Oct 2026, and the page shows no price for those dates. The FAQs & Downloads page quotes add-on rates "valid 1 Oct 2026 – 30 Sep 2027", so a newer tariff apparently exists. **Fix:** The owner should supply the tariff for Nov 2026 onward, and update the validity line and the prices.
- [unclear] “Up to less than 15 days before arrival date or Change in Date during Stay – No Refund.” — "Up to less than" is garbled, and the bands overlap: "Up to 30 days" is a full refund and "Up to 15 days" is 50%. The FAQs page states it differently: 50% for "15–30 days before admission". **Fix:** "30 or more days before admission: full refund. 15–29 days: 50% refund. Fewer than 15 days, or a date change during your stay: no refund." The owner should confirm where the 30-day boundary falls.
- [grammar] “Doctor might change as subject to availability.” — Ungrammatical. **Fix:** "Your physician may change, subject to availability."
- [grammar] “Bring: Cotton clothes, medications, recent reports & open mind” — Missing article. **Fix:** "...recent medical reports and an open mind"
- [typo] “Deep detox with revitalizing therapies.Restore strength, clarity, and vitality.” — Missing space after the full stop (More Programmes card). **Fix:** "...therapies. Restore strength..."
- [typo] “Worlds Luxury Spa Awards Winner 2024” — "Worlds" should be "World". The same error is in "Worlds Luxury Hotel Awards 2024" (title and alt text). Both also contain locked words. **Fix:** "World Luxury Spa Awards 2024", subject to the owner's ruling on whether award names are shown at all.
- [typo] “was honored with the CONSIDICI National Award in 2019.” — The award card heading says "COSIDICI National Award 2019", but the body says "CONSIDICI". **Fix:** "COSIDICI"
- [wrong-fact] “NABH Certified Hospital” — NABH accredits; it does not certify. The page's own awards block says "NABH-accredited for Panchakarma — certificate AH-2024-0250", so the trust strip overstates the scope. The strip appears twice. **Fix:** "NABH-accredited Ayurvedic hospital". The owner should confirm the exact scope wording.
- [overclaim-medical] “Speeds postpartum recovery, reduces fatigue, and balances hormones” — Comparative and hormonal claims stated as fact, with no evidence. **Fix:** "May support postpartum recovery and energy", worded and signed off by Dr. Deepu John.
- [overclaim-medical] “Enhances immunity, supports weight management, and nurtures inner balance” — Unsupported immunity and weight-loss claims made to breastfeeding mothers. **Fix:** Remove, or rephrase with physician approval.
- [overclaim-medical] “Aids in pain relief, boosts emotional well-being, and improves sleep” — "Improves sleep" is not credible for a mother with an infant. "Boosts emotional well-being" implies the programme treats postpartum mood disorders. **Fix:** Needs physician wording, plus a note that postpartum depression needs medical assessment.
- [overclaim-medical] “this treatment aids in healing and restoring your pelvic structure, promoting healthy lactation, reducing stress, and relieving neck and backaches.” — Claims structural pelvic restoration and a lactation effect. **Fix:** Physician to rewrite, for example "therapies aimed at easing back and neck discomfort and supporting lactation".
- [overclaim-medical] “This program focuses on restoring your strength, energy, and shape.” — "Shape" is a body-contouring promise. Together with "regain your pre-pregnancy vitality", it reads as a weight or shape outcome. **Fix:** Drop "shape". Physician to approve the outcome language.
- [overclaim-medical] “Ayurvedic treatments are generally safe and gentle for new mothers.” — A blanket safety claim, and it is the first sentence of the most important FAQ answer. **Fix:** "Treatment is planned only after a physician assesses your delivery, recovery and breastfeeding status." Physician to sign off.
- [overclaim-medical] “ensuring the well-being of both you and your baby.” — Guarantees the infant's safety. The answer also opens "Absolutely." even though herbal medicines can pass into breast milk. **Fix:** "Your physician will choose medicines and oils suitable while breastfeeding and advise on precautions."
- [overclaim-medical] “Ongoing support ensures long-term health and addresses any concerns or needs.” — Guarantees long-term health. **Fix:** "Follow-up consultations help you keep up your diet and routine at home."
- [overclaim-medical] “Renewed Strength and Wellness: Post-Pregnancy Success Stories at Kairali” — The heading promises "Success Stories", plural, under "Transformative Results", but only one testimonial follows. That testimonial attributes an energy gain to the herbal supplements. **Fix:** "What a patient told us". Confirm the patient consented and review the claim.
- [contradiction] “Enjoy a herbal tea or detoxifying drink to kickstart the metabolism.” — The daily routine is the generic detox/weight template: detox drinks at 08:00 and 15:00, and Udwarthana "to promote detoxification". It has no feeding or infant-care time, which contradicts a lactation programme and the page's own breastfeeding FAQ. **Fix:** Replace it with a postpartum-specific day plan approved by the physician that includes nursing breaks.
- [unclear] “(Note: Preventive Health & Regenerative Program includes one therapy per day.)” — A note about a different programme that has leaked into this page's inclusions. **Fix:** Remove.
- [grammar] “Abstinence from Smoking, Alcohol, & Coffee is strictly adhered to.” — Awkward grammar and random capitals. **Fix:** "Smoking, alcohol and coffee are not permitted during treatment."
- [typo] “The above rates are inclusive of applicable taxes*.” — The asterisk has no footnote. **Fix:** Remove the asterisk or add the footnote.

**Must-answer questions missing (11 of 25 gaps):**
- My baby will be about 2–4 months old. Can I bring the baby, and who looks after the baby during my two daily therapies? Is there a cot, a nanny or baby-sitting, and time built in for feeds? — _This is the single biggest blocker for this condition. The page only says under-5s stay free, and the day plan (06:00–22:00) has no room for an infant. Without an answer, a mother cannot picture herself going._ Source: Needs owner (infant-care arrangements, cots, feeding breaks). The FAQs & Downloads page covers only part of it: children are welcome, under-5s are free, and some campus areas are not child-friendly.
- Which herbs, oils and medicines will I be given, and are they safe for my breastfed baby? Will you write to my paediatrician? — _The FAQ answers "Absolutely" without naming anything. International mothers, and their doctors at home, will ask about substances passing into breast milk. This decides whether they book or refuse._ Source: Needs physician (Dr. Deepu John).
- I had a caesarean, or a complicated birth: pre-eclampsia, gestational diabetes, heavy bleeding or anaemia, a stitched tear, diastasis recti. Am I eligible, and why do I have to wait 120 days? — _Caesareans and pregnancy complications are more common in older mothers, including the 40+ IVF mothers who are a realistic audience here. The page gives the 45- and 120-day rule without saying why, or who is excluded._ Source: Needs physician.
- I take postpartum medication: levothyroxine, iron, blood-pressure tablets, an antidepressant, insulin or metformin, the contraceptive pill. Do I continue it? Will the Ayurvedic medicines interact with it? — _The only guidance on the page is "Bring: medications". Patients from Europe, the USA and Russia are usually told by their doctors not to mix herbs with prescription drugs._ Source: Needs physician.
- I have postpartum depression or anxiety. Can I join, and what happens if I struggle emotionally during the stay? Is there a psychiatrist or a referral pathway? — _The page claims the programme "addresses physical, emotional, and mental well-being" and "boosts emotional well-being", but never says where its scope ends or how a mental-health crisis is handled._ Source: Needs physician (scope, screening, referral).
- Can I book while I am still pregnant, and what happens if my due date moves or I end up needing a caesarean, which pushes the earliest admission from 45 to 120 days? — _Booking requires 100% payment in advance, a date change during the stay gets no refund, and the page says nothing about moving dates before arrival. Birth dates are inherently unpredictable, so this policy discourages early booking._ Source: Needs owner (date-change policy for bookings made before the birth). The Cancellation & Refund Policy page has the base rules.
- What exactly happens each day? Which named postpartum therapies, how long does each take, and how is it different from the detox day plan shown? — _The page names only "Sootika Charya" and a massage. The routine shown is a generic detox and weight-loss day, so the patient cannot see what makes this a postpartum programme._ Source: Needs physician (the postpartum therapy protocol and a day plan specific to this programme).
- Will a female physician and female therapists treat me? What are their qualifications, and do any of them speak German, French, Arabic or Russian? — _Patients from the Middle East, and many European patients, need female-only care for postpartum body treatments. The page promises "multilingual staff" but names no languages._ Source: Partly on another site page: Online Consultation lists Dr. Deepu John, Dr. Akhila Oommen and Dr. Rahul R, all BAMS, and FAQs & Downloads explains the BAMS degree. Gender of therapists and languages spoken need owner.
- What will the whole stay cost for 14, 21, 28 or 40 days, including medicines? Are the prices valid for my dates? — _The page gives only per-night villa rates, which expire on 31 Oct 2026, and says medicines "will cost extra" with no range. A mother arriving 45–120 days after birth will almost certainly come after that date._ Source: Needs owner (the Nov 2026+ tariff, a typical range for medicine costs, and whether lactation herbs are included).
- What results should I realistically expect, and how soon? Will my back pain, energy, weight or abdominal separation improve, and what if nothing changes? — _The page promises "shape", "weight management" and "balances hormones" without timelines. A mother aged 40 or over who is paying for international travel needs honest expectations and to know what happens if the programme does not help._ Source: Needs physician (realistic outcomes). Needs owner (policy if the programme does not help).
- Is this a hospital or a holiday resort? Will my insurer, or my doctor at home, take it seriously? — _The same page says "NABH" but also "World Luxury Award Winner", "Wellness Resort of the Year" and "Spa" awards, and "Luxury Stay". The mixed signals undermine clinical trust and insurance reimbursement._ Source: This page (the NABH accreditation block, certificate AH-2024-0250) and the FAQs & Downloads page ("NABH-accredited Ayurvedic hospital... led by BAMS-qualified physicians"). Needs owner to rule on removing the resort, spa and luxury award copy.

**For Dr. Deepu John:** The admission thresholds: treatment "only after 45 days following a normal delivery" and "only after 120 days post-delivery" for a caesarean. These are exact clinical cut-offs published as fact and repeated in the FAQPage JSON-LD. · "Ayurvedic treatments are generally safe and gentle for new mothers." A blanket safety claim. · Breastfeeding FAQ: "Absolutely.", "herbal supplements that support healthy lactation" and "ensuring the well-being of both you and your baby". These claim a lactation effect and guarantee the infant's safety while medicines pass into breast milk. · "aids in healing and restoring your pelvic structure, promoting healthy lactation, reducing stress, and relieving neck and backaches". Structural and lactation outcome claims. · Benefits list: "Speeds postpartum recovery, reduces fatigue, and balances hormones"; "Aids in pain relief, boosts emotional well-being, and improves sleep"; "Enhances immunity, supports weight management". · "restoring your strength, energy, and shape" and "regain your pre-pregnancy vitality". Body-shape and weight outcome claims. · "Holistic post-pregnancy care addresses physical, emotional, and mental well-being". This implies the programme treats postpartum mood disorders; it needs a scope statement and a referral pathway. · Duration: "14/21/28 days, extendable to 40 days" (About) against "two weeks to four weeks" (FAQ). The physician should confirm the real range and the 40-day option. · Naming the programme "Sutika Paricharya" and grounding it in the "Sutika Kala" regimen, while admission starts only after that period ends. The physician must define what the programme is clinically. · The day plan shown: detox drinks "to kickstart the metabolism", Udwarthana "to promote detoxification", 30–45 minutes of asanas at 06:30, and pool and gym access. Is each suitable for lactating and post-caesarean mothers? · History copy: "strengthening the uterus" and "gentle detox therapies" presented as the programme's basis. · "Personalized Herbal Formulations ... address specific post-pregnancy issues". Which formulations, and are they safe while breastfeeding? · "Ongoing support ensures long-term health". A guaranteed-outcome claim. · The testimonial under "Transformative Results" (Mrs. Ankita: "My energy level increased thanks to the food advice and herbal supplements"). The outcome claim needs review, and the patient's consent to publication needs confirming.

### /treatment-for-skin-diseases/ — weak

Chronic and cosmetic skin conditions. The page's own "Who is this for" list covers acne, eczema, psoriasis, dermatitis and allergic reactions, rosacea, hyperpigmentation, dry or dull skin, rashes and itching, skin infections and fungal conditions, sunburn and sun damage, and premature ageing. The stated goal is "skin vitality" through a Panchakarma-based, doctor-guided residential programme ("Ayurveda Skin Vitality Revival Treatment – Tvachā Navīkaraṇa Chikitsa"). The minimum stay is 14 nights (rate reckoner Tier 4).

FAQ questions on the page: 3.

The page neither explains the difference nor links to it, so the patient may book the wrong one._ Source: another site page: /treatment-for-psoriasis/, plus the RATES_RECKONER tiers (Tier 4 vs Tier 5). The triage rule needs physician/owner.
- What exactly will be done to my skin each day: which therapies, how many, and are the Panchakarma procedures included in the price? — _The 'Routine' is a generic daily schedule with one therapy slot (Abhyanga or Udwarthana). The inclusions promise two therapies a day and list Panchakarma as a paid add-on, which contradicts the 'Panchakarma-based' hero._ Source: this page, partly (two therapies a day, generic routine). The skin-specific protocol needs physician/owner, and Panchakarma pricing needs the owner.
- What will the total bill be? How much should I budget for internal medicines, lab tests and extra therapies? — _The page says 'No hidden fees!' but also 'will cost extra', and /faqs-downloads/ q-18 says medicines are included. An international patient needs a firm budget before buying flights._ Source: needs physician/owner. RATES_RECKONER says only 'charged as needed' and gives no indicative amount.
- Who will my doctor be, what are their qualifications and skin experience, and do they speak English, German, French, Arabic or Russian? — _The page claims 'specialized Ayurvedic doctors dedicated to skin diseases' but names nobody. Being understood clinically is a precondition for any foreign patient._ Source: names, BAMS degrees and years of experience: the physician roster (KTAHV homepage, /online-consultation/). Skin experience and spoken languages need physician/owner (/faqs-downloads/ addition A22 is no-recorded-fact).
- Which visa do I need for a 14-night or longer treatment stay, and will you send a medical-visa invitation letter? — _Patients from Europe, the Middle East, the USA and Russia cannot book flights until the visa route is clear. Nothing on the page mentions it._ Source: another site page: /faqs-downloads/ q-36 (visa types) and addition A24 (invitation letter, drafted, not yet signed off)
- How do I get from the airport, how far is it, and what does the transfer cost? — _The page lists 'Airport transfers' under inclusions, but transfers are priced separately. A 60–70-year-old patient needs door-to-door certainty._ Source: another site page: /how-to-reach/ and /faqs-downloads/ q-24 (Coimbatore 75 km, Cochin/Calicut 125 km, priced per vehicle), plus the RATES_RECKONER transfer table
- What deposit do I pay, and do I lose it if my visa is refused, or if the physician finds me unfit on arrival? — _The page says 100% in advance while the reckoner says a 40% deposit, and refunds for visa refusal or medical unfitness are not covered anywhere. This is the biggest financial risk of travelling for treatment._ Source: RATES_RECKONER T&C (40% deposit, balance 30 days before admission) and /cancellation-and-refund-policy/. The visa-refusal and unfit-on-arrival cases need physician/owner (/faqs-downloads/ addition A16 is no-recorded-fact).
- Can my spouse come without being treated, and what do they pay? Does the 'Double' rate mean two patients, or a patient plus a companion? — _Most patients aged 40–70 travel with a partner. The page shows Single and Double rates and an extra bed and meals, but never explains which applies to a companion._ Source: another site page: /faqs-downloads/ additions A5 and A20 (drafted) plus the RATES_RECKONER add-ons. What the Double rate covers needs physician/owner.
- What happens after I go home: medicines to take back, follow-up consultations and their cost, and a report for my dermatologist? — _Chronic skin disease needs months of maintenance. The page mentions a 'Continuous post-discharge wellness plan' but gives no format, frequency or cost._ Source: another site page: /faqs-downloads/ q-39, q-40, q-41 (online follow-ups at 1, 3 and 6 months) and addition A25 (discharge summary). Follow-up and medicine costs need physician/owner.

**For Dr. Deepu John:** FAQ 'Are the therapies and medications safe?' answers with an unqualified 'Yes ... natural and generally safe' and 'to ensure the treatments are suitable'. Dr. Deepu John must rewrite it to cover drug interactions (immunosuppressants, biologics, steroids, anticoagulants), allergies and comorbidities. · 'Panchakarma treatments like Raktamokshana (blood purification) and Virechana (purgation) are very good at cleansing the body and promoting healthy skin' is an efficacy claim. Confirm Raktamokshana is actually offered at KTAHV and state who is excluded (e.g. patients on anticoagulants, with anaemia, diabetes, or advanced age). · The 'Who is This Program For?' eligibility list includes 'Skin infections and fungal conditions', 'Sunburn and sun damage', 'Rosacea and redness' and 'Eczema and psoriasis'. The physician must confirm which are admitted to this 14-night inpatient programme, which go to Psoriasis & Chronic Skin Treatment, and which are declined. · Benefit and outcome promises: 'reduced inflammation and redness ... clearer, healthier complexion', 'your skin gains fortified natural defense mechanisms', 'strengthening immunity—leading to luminous, resilient skin', 'restore the natural glow, elasticity, and health of the skin'. · Durable-outcome claims for chronic relapsing diseases: 'provides lasting relief ... by addressing the root cause of skin diseases', 'aiming for long-lasting relief', 'Ongoing support ensures long-term health', 'maximizes treatment effectiveness for optimal outcomes'. · FAQ duration 'a few weeks to several months' contradicts the 14-night minimum. The physician must set the recommended length by condition and severity. · 'specialized Ayurvedic doctors dedicated to skin diseases': the physician roster shows no published skin specialisation for any of the 5 BAMS physicians. Confirm by name or remove. · Testimonials 'Mr. Priyansh' (psoriasis: 'My skin improved dramatically') and 'Ms Lakshmi' (eczema: 'my skin has never looked better') are disease-outcome testimonials with 5.0 ratings. Confirm they are genuine and consented and that their use is approved. Consider an 'individual results vary' line, and have Indian AYUSH advertising rules checked for disease-outcome testimonials. · 'prepare herbal medicines following Ayurvedic principles for purity and effectiveness' is a purity and effectiveness claim. Confirm the quality-testing basis (e.g. heavy metals) before publishing. · The day-plan therapy examples (Udwarthana dry herbal powder massage, herbal steam baths, oil pulling, warm herbal bath) are generic, not skin-specific. The physician should confirm they suit inflamed, broken or sensitive skin (eczema, rosacea, sunburn) or replace them with skin-appropriate examples. · How often the physician sees the patient: 'Daily Consultation' and 'Daily wellness check-ins with your Ayurvedic doctor' versus 'regular check-ins'. Confirm the actual commitment. · Mechanism framing: 'Ayurveda identifies skin ailments as dosha imbalances', 'removing toxins (Ama) ... treat the underlying causes', 'internal imbalances—often the root cause of skin issues'. These are acceptable only as traditional-theory framing in physician-approved wording, not as established biomedical cause. · All new FAQ answers this audit recommends (medication continuation, flare-ups, comorbidities, expected results and timeline, patch testing, heavy-metal testing, night cover) must be drafted or approved by Dr. Deepu John before staging goes to production (HARD GATE 7).

### /treatment-for-facial-paralysis/ — weak

Facial paralysis (Ardita): Bell's palsy, facial palsy after a stroke, facial nerve injury from trauma or surgery, parotid-related palsy, and facial nerve neuritis. The programme is the residential Facial Paralysis & Nerve Rehabilitation Therapy (Ardita Chikitsa). The rates reckoner (Tier 5) sets a 14-night minimum. The page itself says 14/21/28 days.

FAQ questions on the page: 4.

### /treatment-for-paralysis/ — weak

Paralysis (Pakshāghāta Chikitsa). The page's "Who is this for" list: stroke-induced paralysis, Bell's palsy, traumatic nerve injury, nerve compression, postoperative paralysis, neuritis, muscle weakness/atrophy. The About section also names spinal cord injury, traumatic brain injury, MS, polio, paraplegia and quadriplegia. Stated goal: better mobility, muscle strength, nerve function and quality of life through Ayurvedic therapies, Panchakarma, Rasayana, yoga/physiotherapy and diet. Minimum stay 14 nights, from ₹3,18,920.

FAQ questions on the page: 3.

Screen readers read it out and crawlers index it. It is in the desktop view and in the prerender role=img label. **Fix:** Replace 'a guest' with 'a patient'.

**Must-answer questions missing (11 of 26 gaps):**
- My stroke (or injury or surgery) was X months ago. How soon afterwards is it safe to fly to India and start this programme, and who decides whether I'm ready? — _This is the first safety question every stroke survivor and their family asks. The page has no timing guidance and no contraindications. It lists 'Stroke-induced paralysis' with no acute-phase caveat._ Source: needs physician/owner (Dr. Deepu John). The route to a case-by-case answer already exists: the free pre-programme teleconsultation on this page and /online-consultation/
- I take blood thinners, blood-pressure, diabetes and cholesterol medicines. Will the herbal medicines, purging (Virechana) or steam interact with them? Do I have to stop anything? — _After a stroke, most patients take anticoagulants or antiplatelets and antihypertensives. Herb interactions, and fluid loss from purging, are real fears for a 60–70-year-old. The page says nothing._ Source: /online-consultation/ already says 'continue any care and medication advised by your treating physician; never stop a prescribed medicine without their guidance'. The specific interaction and Virechana-safety answer needs physician
- I have little or no feeling in my weak arm and leg. How do you stop hot Kizhi boluses, Pizhichil oil or steam from burning skin I can't feel, and how do you protect a weak or subluxed shoulder during massage? — _This fear is specific to paralysis: numb limbs, hot therapies, and a fragile hemiplegic shoulder. The page promotes exactly these therapies with no mention of safeguards._ Source: needs physician/owner
- I use a wheelchair or can't transfer, dress or use the toilet alone. Do nurses help day and night, or must a family member do it? — _This decides whether the patient can come at all and whether a carer must travel. The page doesn't mention nursing or help with daily living._ Source: Partial: /faqs-downloads/ ('24-hour assistance', 'ramps and support structures', 'accessible environment'); /how-to-reach/ (wheelchair-accessible vehicle, caregiver can be arranged); the homepage lists assistant nurses; /personalized-customized-healing-program/ claims round-the-clock medical coverage. The actual nursing and daily-living care scope needs owner
- Is there a qualified physiotherapist on staff? How much physiotherapy do I get each day, and is it in the price? — _Stroke patients know rehabilitation depends on physiotherapy. The page mentions 'Gentle physiotherapy exercises' and 'modern techniques', but the package tiles list only 'Daily Group Yoga Sessions'. No page on the site names a physiotherapist._ Source: needs physician/owner
- Will I walk or use my hand again? What improvement is realistic for my type of paralysis and time since onset, and how soon would I notice it? — _This is the core question behind a 14-night, ₹3.2 lakh+ decision made from abroad. The page offers one first-name-only testimonial and outcome words ('restored', 'long-lasting results') with no realistic expectation-setting._ Source: needs physician (no outcome data on the site; must not be invented)
- What will the full 14 nights actually cost me? Are herbal medicines, lab tests, Panchakarma and physiotherapy included, or extra, and roughly how much? — _The page contradicts itself ('All-Inclusive… No hidden fees' against internal medicines, tests and Panchakarma as add-ons) and contradicts /faqs-downloads/ (herbal medicines included). International patients need the total before paying 100% in advance._ Source: needs owner (then reconcile this page with /faqs-downloads/)
- Which conditions can't you help? Complete spinal cord injury, quadriplegia, MS, a progressive disease, or paralysis from many years ago? And is Bell's palsy treated here or on the facial-paralysis programme? — _The About section lists spinal cord injury, MS, polio and quadriplegia, but the eligibility list leaves them out. A patient can't tell whether they'll be turned away on arrival._ Source: needs physician; /treatment-for-facial-paralysis/ for the Bell's palsy routing
- What happens if I have another stroke, a fall or a blood-pressure spike during my stay? Is a doctor on campus at night, and how far is the nearest hospital that can treat a stroke? — _Recurrence risk is real for this group. Families from Europe, the USA and the Gulf judge a rural Kerala campus by its emergency backup._ Source: Partial: /faqs-downloads/ ('arrangements with nearby hospitals and emergency services'; recommends travel insurance with medical evacuation). Night doctor cover, the named stroke-capable hospital and transfer time need owner
- Who are the doctors, what are their qualifications, and do they speak my language? My speech is affected by the stroke, so how will we communicate? — _The page claims doctors 'specialize in neurology', which is not the site's stated credential. 'Multilingual staff' names no languages. Aphasia makes communication a clinical issue._ Source: /online-consultation/ (Dr. Deepu John, Chief Physician & Head of Department, BAMS; Dr. Akhila Oommen, BAMS); /faqs-downloads/ (what the BAMS qualification means). The languages physicians and staff speak, and interpreter availability, need owner
- Does this replace my neurologist and physiotherapy at home, or work alongside them? Will you send reports my doctor can use? — _Patients and their families fear being asked to abandon conventional stroke care. The page says 'combining… with modern techniques' without explaining._ Source: /faqs-downloads/ (admission and discharge notes, treatment summaries and prescriptions for your home doctor). How care is coordinated with the home neurologist needs physician

**For Dr. Deepu John:** 'Our experienced doctors specialize in neurology': credential claim. The site's stated credentials are BAMS (Dr. Deepu John, Chief Physician; Dr. Akhila Oommen). · 'Our therapies and natural remedies stimulate nerve regeneration, reduce inflammation, and promote recovery': mechanism and outcome claim. · 'Specific herbal formulations and Rasayana therapies are used to regenerate nerve tissues': tissue-regeneration claim. · 'Ayurveda addresses the root cause of paralysis for lasting results': root-cause and permanence claim (for stroke the cause is vascular). · Benefits list: 'Improved muscle strength and control, enhanced nerve function', 'restored symmetry', 'long-lasting results': outcome promises. · 'Shirodhara … improves neurological function': outcome claim. · Hero, meta description and og:description: 'Improve mobility & nerve function with holistic healing', plus the programme name 'Paralysis Recovery …': implied outcome. · Testimonial 'My limbs’ strength and sensibility were restored', headed 'Transformative Results' and 'Success Stories': outcome claim through a single, first-name-only testimonial. Needs verification, consent and a results-vary statement. · FAQ 1 labels Abhyangam, Pizhichil and Kizhi as 'Panchakarma treatments', and says they 'enhance muscle strength, nerve function, and overall mobility': wrong classification and outcome claim. The same text is emitted in the FAQPage JSON-LD. · Process step 02 prescribes Virechana (purging) and Swedana, and calls it 'essential for recovery': purging in elderly post-stroke patients on antihypertensives or diuretics is a safety matter the physician must approve. Swedana is misclassified as Panchakarma. Basti/Nasya are not mentioned; the physician should confirm the actual protocol. · FAQ 2: 'Paralysis, according to Ayurveda, is caused by an imbalance in Vata dosha': causal wording should be framed by the physician as Ayurvedic classification, not medical causation. · FAQ 3: 'typically 14 to 28 days or more' and 'promote maximum recovery and long-term benefits': duration and outcome claim. · Process step 06: diet 'focused on boosting nerve health and immunity' and 'prevent further complications': prevention claim that stroke patients will read as preventing recurrence. · 'Who is This Program For?' eligibility list (Bell's palsy, nerve compression, postoperative paralysis, traumatic nerve injury), with no contraindications or timing after an acute stroke: the physician must set inclusion/exclusion criteria and say when after a stroke admission is appropriate. · 'Yoga and Physiotherapy … Gentle physiotherapy exercises' and 'combining ancient Ayurvedic therapies with modern techniques': the physician/owner must confirm physiotherapy is actually delivered and by whom. · Generic Dinacharya timetable on a paralysis page (yoga asanas 30–45 min, nature walk, oil pulling, Udwarthana): the physician should confirm or replace it for hemiplegic patients. · Safety content that is missing but needed on this page: burn risk from hot Kizhi/Pizhichil/steam on limbs with sensory loss, handling of the hemiplegic shoulder, fall risk after oil therapies, and herb interactions with anticoagulants. Any answer written for these needs Dr. Deepu John's sign-off.

### /treatment-for-cervical-spondylosis/ — weak

Cervical spondylosis: age-related wear of the neck (cervical spine) discs and joints, causing neck pain, stiffness and reduced mobility. The H1 is "Neck & Upper Back Rehabilitation Treatment" (Manyastambha Chikitsa). For the sitemap flow, the body copy drifts beyond that scope. It says "neck and back pain", lists "Relief from Shoulder Pain", and calls the condition "Spondylitis" in several places. That spreads the page into the areas covered by /treatment-for-spondylitis/ (Spine Care & Spondylitis), /treatment-for-chronic-back-pain/ and /treatment-for-slip-disc/. The Process, Benefits and "Why opt" sections are the generic preventive-programme template and say nothing specific to the neck. Only About, History, the hero and the single testimonial are actually about this condition.

FAQ questions on the page: 0.

**Proofreading (verified):**
- [grammar] “we offer a Ayurvedic treatment for neck and back pain to treating this condition.” — Three errors: 'a Ayurvedic' should be 'an Ayurvedic'; 'to treating' is ungrammatical; and 'neck and back pain' goes beyond the programme's own scope (neck and upper back / cervical spondylosis). **Fix:** "...we offer an Ayurvedic treatment programme for cervical spondylosis and the neck and upper-back pain it causes."
- [contradiction] “For over 115 years, Kairali has been at the forefront of providing holistic and authentic Ayurvedic treatments” — This conflicts with the trust strip ('118+ Years Heritage') and the footer ('since 1908'), which give 118 years in 2026. The 115 is stale. **Fix:** Use "Since 1908" so the number cannot go out of date, or change it to 118 so it matches the trust strip.
- [locked-word] “Those interested in curing Cervical” — 'curing' is a banned word under the KTAHV lock, and it also implies the condition can be cured. The phrase is incomplete ('Cervical' what?). **Fix:** "Patients with cervical spondylosis who want to manage neck pain and stiffness" (wording for physician sign-off).
- [wrong-fact] “Patients looking for natural therapy for Spondylitis” — Wrong condition. Spondylitis is inflammatory (for example ankylosing spondylitis). Spondylosis is degenerative, which is what this page describes. Spondylitis has its own page, /treatment-for-spondylitis/, which is linked below, so the two pages blur together. **Fix:** "Patients with cervical spondylosis looking for Ayurvedic, non-surgical care"
- [wrong-fact] “Empowering Recovery: Testimonials on Kairali’s Holistic Approach to Cervical Spondylitis” — The heading says 'Spondylitis', but the testimonial below it says 'cervical spondylosis'. The heading also says 'Testimonials' (plural) when there is only one. The image alt 'Ayurvedic Doctor Consultation for Cervical Spondylitis' has the same wrong condition name. **Fix:** "A patient's experience: cervical spondylosis care at Kairali". Also correct the alt text to 'spondylosis'.
- [typo] “Neck & Upper Back Rehabilitation TreatmentManyastambha Chikitsa  (Kairali’s Treatment for Cervical Spondylitis)” — This is the og:title in route-manifest.json metadata. Two strings are joined with no space ('TreatmentManyastambha'), there is a double space, and it names the wrong condition ('Spondylitis'). **Fix:** "Neck & Upper Back Rehabilitation Treatment – Manyastambha Chikitsa (Kairali’s Treatment for Cervical Spondylosis)"
- [unclear] “Relief from Shoulder Pain” — This item sits under 'Who is This Program For?' but is not a group of people. It also widens the scope to shoulder pain, which can have causes unrelated to cervical spondylosis. **Fix:** "Patients whose neck pain spreads to the shoulders or upper back" (physician to confirm the scope).
